# Eastern Orthodox Church

This article is about the Eastern Orthodox Church as an institution. For its religion, doctrine and tradition, see [Eastern Orthodoxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodoxy).

For other uses of "Orthodox Church", see [Orthodox Church (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Church_\(disambiguation\)).

Not to be confused with [Oriental Orthodox Churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriental_Orthodox_Churches), [Eastern Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Christianity), or [Eastern Catholic Churches](214903.md).

| Members | 220,266,000 (2020)[^cite_note-\:3-9] |
| Other names | Orthodox Church, Orthodox Christian Church, Eastern Orthodox Church |

*[Christ Pantocrator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christ_Pantocrator_\(Sinai\))*, sixth century, [Saint Catherine's Monastery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Catherine's_Monastery), [Sinai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinai_Peninsula); the oldest known [icon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Icon) of Christ, in one of the oldest monasteries in the world

The **Eastern Orthodox Church**, officially the **Orthodox Catholic Church**,[^cite_note-Britannica-10][^cite_note-ellwood-11][^cite_note-tsichlis-12] also known as the **Greek Orthodox Church**[^cite_note-EBGreek-13] is the second-largest Christian body in the world after the [Roman Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Church),[^cite_note-Adherents3-14][^cite_note-15][^cite_note-\:3-9] and one of the [three major doctrinal and jurisdictional groups of Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Christian_denominations_by_number_of_members).[^cite_note-Britannica-10]

The Eastern Orthodox Church operates as a [communion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communion_\(Christian\)) of [autocephalous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autocephalous) churches, each governed by its [bishops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bishop_\(Orthodox_Church\)) via local [synods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Synod).[^cite_note-BBC1-16] The church has no central doctrinal or governmental authority analogous to the [pope](23056.md) of the Roman Catholic Church. Nevertheless, the [Ecumenical Patriarch of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecumenical_Patriarch_of_Constantinople) is recognised by them as *[primus inter pares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primus_inter_pares)* ('first among equals'),[^cite_note-aboutWeb-17][^cite_note-18][^cite_note-19][^cite_note-20] a title held by the [patriarch of Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriarch_of_Rome) prior to the [East–West Schism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East%E2%80%93West_Schism).

As one of the oldest surviving religious institutions in the world, the Eastern Orthodox Church has played an especially prominent role in the history and culture of [Eastern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Europe) and [Southeastern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeastern_Europe).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWare19938-21] Since 2018, there has been an [ongoing schism between Constantinople and Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Moscow%E2%80%93Constantinople_schism), with the two not in [full communion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Full_communion) with each other.

[Eastern Orthodox theology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_theology) is based on the [Scriptures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scriptures) and [holy tradition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_tradition), which incorporates the dogmatic decrees of the [seven ecumenical councils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_ecumenical_councils), and the teaching of the [Church Fathers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_Fathers). The church teaches that it is the [one, holy, catholic and apostolic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One,_holy,_catholic_and_apostolic) [church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_true_church) established by [Jesus Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_in_Christianity) in his [Great Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Commission),[^cite_note-OCA-22] and that its bishops are the [successors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostolic_succession) of Christ's [apostles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostles_in_the_New_Testament).[^cite_note-23] It maintains that it practises the original Christian faith, as passed down by holy tradition. Its [patriarchates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriarchates), descending from the [pentarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentarchy), and other autocephalous and [autonomous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomy_\(Eastern_Orthodoxy\)) churches, reflect a variety of [hierarchical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hierarchical) [organisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church_organization). It recognises seven major [sacraments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacrament) (which are called [holy mysteries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_mysteries_\(Orthodox_Church\))), of which the [Eucharist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eucharist) is the principal one, celebrated [liturgically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divine_Liturgy) in [synaxis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synaxis). The church teaches that through [consecration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consecration#Eucharist) [invoked](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epiclesis) by a [priest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Priesthood_\(Orthodox_Church\)), the sacrificial bread and wine become the body and blood of Christ. The [Virgin Mary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virgin_Mary) is [venerated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venerated) in the Eastern Orthodox Church as the *[Theotokos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theotokos)*, which means 'God-bearer', and she is honoured in [devotions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marian_devotions).

The churches of [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecumenical_Patriarchate_of_Constantinople), [Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Patriarchate_of_Alexandria), [Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Patriarchate_of_Jerusalem), and [Antioch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Patriarchate_of_Antioch)—except for some breaks of communion such as the [Photian schism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Photian_schism) or the [Acacian schism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acacian_schism)—[shared communion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koinonia#Between_churches) with the [Church of Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diocese_of_Rome) until the [East–West Schism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East%E2%80%93West_Schism) in 1054. The 1054 schism was the culmination of mounting theological, political, and cultural disputes, particularly over the [authority of the pope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papal_supremacy), between those churches. Before the [Council of Ephesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Ephesus) in AD 431, the [Church of the East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_the_East) also shared in this communion, as did the various [Oriental Orthodox Churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriental_Orthodox_Churches) before the [Council of Chalcedon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Chalcedon) in AD 451, all separating primarily over [differences in](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chalcedonian_Definition) [Christology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christology).

The Eastern Orthodox Church is the primary religious confession in [Russia](25391.md), [Ukraine](31750.md), [Romania](25445.md), [Greece](12108.md), [Belarus](3457.md), [Serbia](29265.md), [Bulgaria](3415.md), [Georgia](48768.md), [Moldova](19260.md), [North Macedonia](23564616.md), [Cyprus](5593.md), and [Montenegro](20760.md). Eastern Orthodox Christians are also one of the main religious groups in [Estonia](28222445.md), [Bosnia and Herzegovina](3463.md), [Kosovo](17391.md) and [Latvia](17514.md) as well as a significant group in [Syria](7515849.md), [Lebanon](17771.md), and other countries in the [Middle East](19323.md). Roughly half of Eastern Orthodox Christians live in the post Eastern Bloc countries, mostly in Russia.[^cite_note-24][^cite_note-25] The communities in the former [Byzantine regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire) of [North Africa](21714.md) and the [Eastern Mediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Mediterranean) are among the oldest Orthodox communities from the Middle East, which are decreasing due to forced migration driven by increased [religious persecution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Christians).[^cite_note-Guardian-26][^cite_note-K4D-27] Eastern Orthodox communities outside [Western Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Asia), [Asia Minor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asia_Minor), [Caucasia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus) and [Eastern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Europe), including those in [North America](21139.md), [Western Europe](33800.md), and [Australia](4689264.md), have been formed through [diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diaspora), [conversions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conversion_to_Christianity), and [missionary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missionary) activity.

## Name and characteristics

### Definition

The Eastern Orthodox Church is defined as the [Eastern Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Christians) which recognise the [seven ecumenical councils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_ecumenical_councils) and usually are in [communion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koinonia) with the [Ecumenical Patriarchate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecumenical_Patriarchate), the [Patriarchate of Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Church_of_Alexandria), the [Patriarchate of Antioch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Church_of_Antioch), and the [Patriarchate of Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Church_of_Jerusalem). The Eastern Orthodox churches "are defined positively by their adherence to the [dogmatic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dogma) definitions of the seven \[ecumenical] councils, by the strong sense of not being a [sect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sect) or a [denomination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_denomination) but simply continuing the [Christian church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_true_church), and, despite their varied origins, by adherence to the [Byzantine rite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_rite)". Those churches are negatively defined by their rejection of [papal immediate and universal supremacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papal_supremacy).[^cite_note-28]

The seven ecumenical councils recognised by the Eastern Orthodox churches are: [Nicaea I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicaea_I), [Constantinople I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Council_of_Constantinople), [Ephesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Ephesus), [Chalcedon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Chalcedon), [Constantinople II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Council_of_Constantinople), [Constantinople III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Council_of_Constantinople), and [Nicaea II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicaea_II).[^cite_note-OC-29][^cite_note-EC-30] These churches consider the [Quinisext Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quinisext_Council) as "shar\[ing] the ecumenical authority of Constantinople III."[^cite_note-EC-30] "By an agreement that appears to be in place in the \[Eastern] Orthodox world, possibly the [council held in 879](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourth_Council_of_Constantinople_\(Eastern_Orthodox\)) to vindicate the [Patriarch Photius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Photios_I_of_Constantinople) will at some future date be recognized as the eighth \[ecumenical] council" by the Eastern Orthodox Church.[^cite_note-OC-29]

[Western Rite Orthodoxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Rite_Orthodoxy) exists both outside and inside [Eastern Orthodoxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodoxy). Within Eastern Orthodoxy, it is practised by a [vicariate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vicariate) of the Antiochian Orthodox church.[^cite_note-31] The [Antiochian Orthodox Archdiocese of North America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antiochian_Orthodox_Christian_Archdiocese_of_North_America) estimated there are 1,416 members of the Western Rite vicariate in 24 parishes.[^cite_note-32] With roughly 3,000 members worldwide, spread across about 50 scattered [parishes](23623.md), Western Rite Orthodoxy remains a very small minority within the Eastern Orthodox Church.[^cite_note-33]

### Name

The official name of the Eastern Orthodox Church is the "Orthodox Catholic Church".[^cite_note-Britannica-10][^cite_note-ellwood-11][^cite_note-tsichlis-12][^cite_note-Losch2002-34] It is the name by which the church refers to itself[^cite_note-35][^cite_note-36][^cite_note-37][^cite_note-38][^cite_note-39][^cite_note-40] and which is issued in its liturgical or [canonical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canon_law_of_the_Eastern_Orthodox_Church) texts.[^cite_note-41][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMerriam-Webster_Encyclopedia_of_World_Religions1999\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidZP_f9icf2roCpg309_309\]:_"The_official_designation_of_the_church_in_Eastern_Orthodox_liturgical_or_canonical_texts_is_'the_Orthodox_Catholic_Church.<span_style="padding-right:.15em;">'</span>"-42] Eastern Orthodox theologians refer to the church as Catholic.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWare1993307-43][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFitzgerald1998\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid_F5yntZocGICpgPA8_8\]-44] This name and longer variants containing "Catholic" are also recognised and referenced in other books and publications by secular or non-Eastern Orthodox writers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDe_Vie1945-45][^cite_note-FOOTNOTENielsenJohnsonEllis2001248_"In_the_Eastern_Orthodox_Catholic_Church,_authority"-46][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFortescue2008\[httpsarchiveorgdetailsbub_gb_UPr1ZCxPW6QCpagen290_255\]_"it_is_all_gathered_together_and_still_lives_in_the_Holy_Apostolic_Orthodox_Catholic_Church_of_the_Seven_Councils."-47][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchadé_Encyclopedia_of_World_Religions2006-48][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELosch2002\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidOdKCVjCschMCpgPA76_76\]-49][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMerriam-Webster_Encyclopedia_of_World_Religions1999\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidZP_f9icf2roCpg309_309–310\]-50] The [catechism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catechism) of [Philaret (Drozdov) of Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philaret_\(Drozdov\)_of_Moscow) published in the 19th century is titled: *The Longer Catechism of the Orthodox, Catholic, Eastern Church*[^cite_note-51] ([Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_language): Пространный христианский катехизис православныя, кафолическия восточныя Церкви).

In keeping with the church's teaching on universality and with the Nicene Creed, Eastern Orthodox authorities such as [Raphael of Brooklyn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raphael_of_Brooklyn) have insisted that the full name of the church has always included the term "[Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_\(term\))", as in "Holy [Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodoxy) Catholic [Apostolic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelve_apostles) [Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Church)".[^cite_note-52][^cite_note-53]

From ancient times through the first millennium, Greek was the most prevalent shared language in the demographic regions where the [Byzantine Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire) flourished, and Greek, being the language in which the [New Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Testament) was written, was the primary liturgical language of the church. For this reason, the eastern churches were sometimes identified as "Greek" (in contrast to the ["Roman" or "Latin" church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_Church), which used a Latin translation of the Bible), even before the Great Schism of 1054. After 1054, "Greek Orthodox" or "Greek Catholic" marked a church as being in communion with Constantinople, much as "Catholic" did for communion with the [Catholic Church](606848.md).[^cite_note-EBGreek-13]

In Hungarian, the church is still commonly called "Eastern Greek" ([Hungarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hungarian_language): *Görögkeleti*). This identification with Greek, however, became increasingly confusing with time. Missionaries brought Eastern Orthodoxy to many regions without ethnic Greeks, where the Greek language was not spoken. In addition, struggles between Rome and Constantinople to control parts of [Southeastern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeastern_Europe) resulted in the conversion of some churches to the Catholic Church, which then also used "Greek Catholic" to indicate their continued use of the Byzantine rites. Today, only a minority of Eastern Orthodox adherents use Greek as the language of worship.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEncyclopædia_Britannica_Online\[httpswwwbritannicacomEBcheckedtopic177174Eastern-Orthodoxy_"Eastern_Orthodoxy"\]_"Because_of_the_use_of_the_name_'Greek_Catholics'_by_the_Eastern_churches_of_the_Catholic_Church_and_the_historical_links_of_the_Orthodox_Catholic_church_with_the_Eastern_Roman_Empire_and_Byzantium_(Constantinople),_however,_the_\[\[exonym\]\]s_in_American_English_usage_referred_to_it_as_the_'Eastern'_or_'Greek_Orthodox'_Church._These_terms_are_sometimes_misleading,_especially_when_applied_to_Russian_or_Slavic_churches_and_to_the_Orthodox_communities_in_western_Europe_and_America."-54]

"Eastern", then, indicates the geographical element in the church's origin and development, while "Orthodox" indicates the faith, as well as communion with the Ecumenical Patriarchate of Constantinople.[^cite_note-Calivas1983-55] There are additional Christian churches in the east that are in communion with neither the Catholic Church nor the Eastern Orthodox Church, who tend to be distinguished by the category named "[Oriental Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriental_Orthodox)".[^cite_note-56] While the Eastern Orthodox Church continues officially to call itself "Catholic", for reasons of [universality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholicity), the common title of "Eastern Orthodox Church" avoids casual confusion with the Catholic Church.[^cite_note-57][^cite_note-58]

### Orthodoxy



[Emperor Constantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantine_the_Great) presents a representation of the city of [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople) as tribute to an enthroned Mary and baby Jesus in this church mosaic ([Hagia Sophia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hagia_Sophia), c. 1000).

The first known use of the phrase "the catholic Church" (*he katholike ekklesia*) occurred in a letter written c. AD 110 from one Greek church to another ([Ignatius of Antioch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ignatius_of_Antioch) to the [Smyrnaeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smyrna)). The letter states: "Wheresoever the bishop shall appear, there let the people be, even as where Jesus may be, there is the universal \[katholike] Church."[^cite_note-59] Thus, almost from the beginning, Christians referred to the Christian Church as the "one, holy, catholic (from the Greek καθολική, 'according to the whole, universal')[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHardon1981\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid3Ke37zpSv3gCpgPA217_217\]-60] and apostolic Church".[^cite_note-OCA-22] The Eastern Orthodox Church claims that it is today the continuation and preservation of that same early church.

A number of other Christian churches also make a similar claim: the [Catholic Church](606848.md), the [Anglican Communion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglican_Communion), the [Church of the East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrian_Church_of_the_East), and the [Oriental Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriental_Orthodoxy). In the Eastern Orthodox view, the Church of the East and Oriental Orthodox left the Orthodox Church in the years following the Third Ecumenical [Council of Ephesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Ephesus) (431) and the Fourth Ecumenical [Council of Chalcedon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Chalcedon) (451), respectively, in their refusal to accept those councils' [Christological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christological) definitions; conversely, the latter two churches hold the same view towards the Eastern Orthodox Church regarding each respective council. Similarly, the churches in Rome and Constantinople separated in an event known as the [East–West Schism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East%E2%80%93West_Schism), traditionally dated to the year 1054, although it was more a gradual process than a sudden break.

To all these churches, the claim to [catholicity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholicity) (universality, oneness with the ancient Church) is important for multiple doctrinal reasons that have more bearing internally in each church than in their relation to the others, now separated in faith. The meaning of holding to a faith that is true is the primary reason why anyone's statement of which church split off from which other has any significance at all; the issues go as deep as the schisms. The depth of this meaning in the Eastern Orthodox Church is registered first in its use of the word "[Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodoxy)" itself, a union of [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language) *orthos* ("straight", "correct", "true", "right") and *[doxa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doxa)* ("common belief", from the ancient verb δοκέω-δοκῶ which is translated "to believe", "to think", "to consider", "to imagine", "to assume").[^cite_note-61]

The dual meanings of *doxa*, with "glory" or "glorification" (of God by the church and of the church by God), especially in worship, yield the pair "correct belief" and "true worship". Together, these express the core of a fundamental teaching about the inseparability of belief and worship and their role in drawing the church together with Christ.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWare199116,_271-62][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHierotheos199869–72-63] All [Slavic Orthodox churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavic_Orthodox_churches) use the title *Pravoslavie* ([Cyrillic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyrillic_script): Православие), meaning "correctness of glorification", to denote what is in English *Orthodoxy*, while the Georgians use the title *Martlmadidebeli* ([Georgian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_language): მართლმადიდებელი).[^cite_note-64]

The term "Eastern Church" (the geographic east in the East–West Schism) has been used to distinguish it from [western Christendom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Christendom) (the geographic West, which at first came to designate the Catholic communion, later also the various [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant) and Anglican branches). "Eastern" is used to indicate that the highest concentrations of the Eastern Orthodox Church presence remain in the eastern part of the Christian world, although it is growing worldwide. Orthodox Christians throughout the world use various ethnic or national jurisdictional titles, or more inclusively, the title "Eastern Orthodox", "Orthodox Catholic", or simply "Orthodox".[^cite_note-Calivas1983-55]

What unites Orthodox Christians is the catholic faith as carried through [holy tradition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_tradition). That faith is expressed most fundamentally in scripture and worship,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWare1991212–213-65] and the latter most essentially through baptism and in the [Divine Liturgy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divine_Liturgy).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWare1991282-66]

The lines of even this test can blur, however, when differences that arise are not due to doctrine, but to recognition of jurisdiction. As the Eastern Orthodox Church has spread into the West and over the world, the church as a whole has yet to sort out all the inter-jurisdictional issues that have arisen in the expansion, leaving some areas of doubt about what is proper church governance.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWare1991180–199-67] Moreover, as in the ancient church persecutions, the aftermath of [persecutions of Christians in communist nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Christians_in_the_Soviet_Union) has complicated some issues of governance that have yet to be completely resolved.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWare1991152–179-68]

All members of the Eastern Orthodox Church profess the same faith, regardless of race or nationality, jurisdiction or local custom, or century of birth. Holy tradition encompasses the understandings and means by which that unity of faith is transmitted across boundaries of time, geography, and culture. It is a continuity that exists only inasmuch as it lives within Christians themselves.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWare1991203–204-69] It is not static, nor an observation of rules, but rather a sharing of observations that spring both from within and also in keeping with others, even others who lived lives long past. The church proclaims the Holy Spirit maintains the unity and consistency of holy tradition to preserve the integrity of the faith within the church, as given in the scriptural promises.[^cite_note-70]

Orthodoxy asserts that its shared beliefs, and its theology, exist within holy tradition and cannot be separated from it, and that their meaning is not expressed in mere words alone;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWare1991215-71] that doctrine cannot be understood unless it is prayed;[^cite_note-72] and that it must also be lived in order to be prayed, that without action, the prayer is idle, empty, and in vain, and therefore the theology of demons.[^cite_note-73]

### Catholicity



Eastern Orthodox [icon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Icon) of St. [John the Baptist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_the_Baptist) by [Emmanuel Tzanes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emmanuel_Tzanes), 17th century

The Eastern Orthodox Church considers itself to be both orthodox and catholic.[^cite_note-74] The doctrine of the [Catholicity of the Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholicity_of_the_Church), as derived from the [Nicene Creed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicene_Creed), is essential to Eastern Orthodox [ecclesiology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecclesiology). The term *Catholicity of the Church* ([Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language): Καθολικότης τῆς Ἐκκλησίας) is used in its original sense, as a designation for the universality of the Christian Church, centred around Christ. Therefore, the Eastern Orthodox notion of catholicity is not centred around any singular see, unlike the Catholic Church which has one earthly centre.

Due to the influence of the Catholic Church in the West, where the [English language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language) itself developed, the words "catholic" and "catholicity" are sometimes used to refer to that church specifically. However, the more prominent dictionary sense given for general use is still the one shared by other languages, implying breadth and universality, reflecting comprehensive scope.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmerican_Heritage_Dict5th_ed294_\[httpahdictionarycomwordsearchhtmlqcatholicity_"catholicity"\]-75] In a Christian context, the Christian Church, as identified with the original church founded by Christ and his apostles, is said to be catholic (or universal) in regard to its union with Christ in faith.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWare199116-76]

Just as Christ is indivisible, so are union with him and faith in him, whereby the Christian Church is "universal", unseparated, and comprehensive, including all who share that faith. Orthodox bishop [Kallistos Ware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kallistos_Ware) has called that "simple Christianity".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWare199116-76] That is the sense of early and [patristic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patristic) usage wherein the church usually refers to itself as the "Catholic Church",[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEncyclopedia_of_Christianity2003\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid7ly4DgtT3LkCpgPA867_867\]-77][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELeith1982\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidsaFgJRjaPwcCpgPA486_486\]-78] whose faith is the "Orthodox faith". It is also the sense within the phrase "one, holy, catholic, and apostolic Church", found in the [Nicene Creed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicene_Creed), and referred to in Orthodox worship, e.g. in the litany of the catechumens in the Divine Liturgy.

With the mutual excommunications of the East–West Schism in 1054,[^cite_note-79] the churches in Rome and Constantinople each viewed the other as having departed from the [true church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_true_church), leaving a smaller but still-catholic church in place. Each retained the "Catholic" part of its title, the "*Roman* Catholic Church" (or Catholic Church) on the one hand, and the "*Orthodox* Catholic Church" on the other, each of which was defined in terms of inter-communion with either Rome or Constantinople. While the Eastern Orthodox Church recognises what it shares in common with other churches, including the Catholic Church, it sees catholicity in terms of complete union in communion and faith, with the Church throughout all time, and the sharing remains incomplete when not shared fully.

## History

Main article: [History of the Eastern Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Eastern_Orthodox_Church)

### Early Church



An early Christian "[Ichthys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ichthys)" (*fish*) inscription from ancient [Ephesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ephesus)

[Paul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_of_Tarsus) and the [Apostles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelve_Apostles) travelled extensively throughout the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire), including Asia Minor, [establishing churches in major communities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Christianity), with the first churches appearing in [Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_in_Christianity) and the [Holy Land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Land), then in [Antioch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antioch), [Ethiopia](187749.md), [Egypt](8087628.md), [Rome](25458.md), [Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandria), [Athens](1216.md), [Thessalonica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessalonica), [Illyricum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illyricum_\(Roman_province\)), and [Byzantium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople), which centuries later would become prominent as the [New Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Rome).[^cite_note-ware1993-80] Christianity [encountered considerable resistance in the Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Christians_in_the_Roman_Empire), mostly because its adherents refused to comply with the demands of the Roman state—often even when their lives were threatened—by offering sacrifices to the pagan gods. Despite persecution, scepticism, and initial social stigma, the Christian Church spread, particularly following the [conversion of Emperor Constantine I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantine_I_and_Christianity) in AD 312.[^cite_note-ware1993-80]

By the [fourth century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_the_4th_century), Christianity was present in numerous regions well beyond the Levant. A number of influential schools of thought had arisen, particularly the [Alexandrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catechetical_School_of_Alexandria) and [Antiochian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_of_Antioch) philosophical approaches. Other groups, such as the [Arians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arians), had also managed to gain influence. However, their positions caused theological conflicts within the church, thus prompting the [Emperor Constantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantine_the_Great) to call for a great ecumenical synod to define the church's position against the growing, often widely diverging, philosophical and theological interpretations of Christianity. He made it possible for this council to meet not only by providing a location but also by offering to pay for the transportation of all the existing bishops of the church. Most modern Christian churches regard this synod, commonly called the [First Council of Nicaea](11118.md) or more generally the [First Ecumenical Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Ecumenical_Council),[^cite_note-ware1993-80][^cite_note-81] as of major importance.

### Ecumenical councils

Main article: [First seven Ecumenical Councils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_seven_Ecumenical_Councils)

See also: [State church of the Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_church_of_the_Roman_Empire)



[Icon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Icon) depicting [Constantine the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantine_the_Great), accompanied by the bishops of the [First Council of Nicaea](11118.md) (325), holding the [Niceno-Constantinopolitan Creed of 381](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niceno-Constantinopolitan_Creed). First line of main text in Greek: Πιστεύω εἰς ἕνα Θ\[εό]ν, πατέρα παντοκράτορα, ποιητὴν οὐρανοῦ κ\[αὶ] γῆς. Translation: "I believe in one God, Father Almighty, Creator of heaven and earth."

Several doctrinal disputes from the fourth century onwards led to the calling of [ecumenical councils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecumenical_council). In the Orthodox Church, an ecumenical council is the supreme authority that can be invoked to resolve contested issues of the faith. As such, these councils have been held to resolve the most important theological matters that came to be disputed within the Christian Church. Many lesser disagreements were resolved through local councils in the areas where they arose, before they grew significant enough to require an ecumenical council.

There are seven councils authoritatively recognised as ecumenical by the Eastern Orthodox Church:

1. The [First Ecumenical Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Ecumenical_Council) was convoked by the Roman Emperor Constantine at [Nicaea](77588.md) in 325 and presided over by the Patriarch [Alexander of Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Alexander_I_of_Alexandria), with over 300 bishops condemning the view of [Arius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arius) that the Son is a created being inferior to the Father.

   [^cite_note-82]

2. The [Second Ecumenical Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Ecumenical_Council) was held at Constantinople in 381, presided over by the Patriarchs of Alexandria and Antioch, with 150 bishops, defining the nature of the Holy Spirit against those asserting His inequality with the other persons of the Trinity.

   [^cite_note-83]

3. The [Third Ecumenical Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Ecumenical_Council) is that of Ephesus in 431, presided over by the Patriarch of Alexandria, with 250 bishops, which affirmed that Mary is truly "Birthgiver" or "Mother" of God (*[Theotokos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theotokos)*), contrary to the teachings of [Nestorius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nestorius).

   [^cite_note-84]

4. The [Fourth Ecumenical Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourth_Ecumenical_Council) is that of Chalcedon in 451, Patriarch of Constantinople presiding, 500 bishops, affirmed that Jesus is truly God and truly man, without mixture of the two natures, contrary to [Monophysite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monophysite) teaching.

   [^cite_note-85]

5. The [Fifth Ecumenical Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fifth_Ecumenical_Council) is the second of Constantinople in 553, interpreting the decrees of Chalcedon and further explaining the relationship of the two natures of Jesus; it also condemned the alleged teachings of [Origen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Origen) on the pre-existence of the soul, etc.

   [^cite_note-86]

6. The [Sixth Ecumenical Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sixth_Ecumenical_Council) is the third of Constantinople in 681; it declared that Christ has two wills of his two natures, human and divine, contrary to the teachings of the [Monothelites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monothelites).

   [^cite_note-87]

7. The [Seventh Ecumenical Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seventh_Ecumenical_Council) was called under the Empress Regent [Irene of Athens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irene_of_Athens) in 787, known as the second of Nicaea. It supports the [veneration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Veneration) of [icons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Icon) while forbidding their worship. It is often referred to as "The Triumph of Orthodoxy".

   [^cite_note-88]

There are also two other councils which are considered ecumenical by some Eastern Orthodox:

- The [Fourth Council of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourth_Council_of_Constantinople_\(Eastern_Orthodox\)) was called in 879. It restored [Photius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Photius) to his [See in Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecumenical_Patriarchate_of_Constantinople) and condemned any alteration of the Nicene-Constantinopolitan Creed of 381.

- The [Fifth Council of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fifth_Council_of_Constantinople) was actually a series of councils held between 1341 and 1368. It affirmed the [hesychastic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hesychastic) theology of [Gregory Palamas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gregory_Palamas) and condemned the philosopher [Barlaam of Calabria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barlaam_of_Calabria).

### Other major councils

In addition to these councils, there have been a number of other significant councils meant to further define the Eastern Orthodox position. They are the Synods of Constantinople, in [1484](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synod_of_Constantinople_\(1484\)), 1583, [1755](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oros_of_the_Holy_Great_Church_of_Christ), 1819, and [1872](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phyletism), the [Synod of Iași](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synod_of_Ia%C8%99i) in 1642, and the Pan-Orthodox [Synod of Jerusalem in 1672](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synod_of_Jerusalem_\(1672\)). Another council convened in [June 2016](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Orthodox_Council) to discuss many modern phenomena, other Christian confessions, Eastern Orthodoxy's relation with other religions and fasting disciplines.[^cite_note-89]

### Roman and Byzantine Empire

Main articles: [State church of the Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_church_of_the_Roman_Empire) and [Ecumenical Patriarchate of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecumenical_Patriarchate_of_Constantinople)



The [Hagia Sophia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hagia_Sophia), the largest church in the world and patriarchal basilica of [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople) for nearly a thousand years, later converted into a [mosque](19894.md), then a museum, then back to a mosque

[Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople) is generally considered to be the centre and the "cradle of Orthodox [Christian civilisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Role_of_Christianity_in_civilisation)".[^cite_note-90][^cite_note-91] From the mid-5th century to the early 13th century, Constantinople was the largest and wealthiest city in Europe.[^cite_note-92] Eastern [Christian culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_culture) reached its golden age during the high point of the [Byzantine Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire) and continued to flourish in Ukraine and Russia, after the [fall of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Constantinople). Numerous [autocephalous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autocephalous) churches were established in Europe: Greece, Georgia, Ukraine, as well as in Russia and Asia.

In the 530s the [Church of the Holy Wisdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_the_Holy_Wisdom) (Hagia Sophia) was built in [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople) under Emperor [Justinian I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justinian_I).[^cite_note-93] Beginning with subsequent [Byzantine architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_architecture), Hagia Sophia became the paradigmatic [Orthodox church form](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_church_architecture) and its architectural style was emulated by [Ottoman mosques](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_architecture#Classical_period_\(1437%E2%80%931703\)) a thousand years later.[^cite_note-Heinle1996-94][^cite_note-95] Being the [episcopal see](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Episcopal_see) of the [ecumenical patriarch of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecumenical_patriarch_of_Constantinople), it remained the world's largest cathedral for nearly a thousand years, until [Seville Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seville_Cathedral) was completed in 1520. [Hagia Sophia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hagia_Sophia) has been described as "holding a unique position in the [Christian world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_world)",[^cite_note-Heinle1996-94] and architectural and [cultural icon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_icon) of [Byzantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_culture) and [Eastern Orthodox civilisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Culture),[^cite_note-96][^cite_note-M19-97] and it is considered the epitome of Byzantine architecture[^cite_note-98] and is said to have "changed the history of architecture".[^cite_note-nytimes22-99]

### Early schisms



15th-century mural from [Ferapontov Monastery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferapontov_Monastery) depicting the deposition of [Nestorius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nestorius) at the [Council of Ephesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Ephesus) (431).

There are the "[Nestorian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nestorian)" churches resulted from the reaction of the [Council of Ephesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Ephesus) (431), which are the earliest surviving Eastern Christian churches that keep the faith of only the first two ecumenical councils, i.e., the [First Council of Nicaea](11118.md) (325) and the [First Council of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Council_of_Constantinople) (381) as legitimate. "Nestorian" is an outsider's term for a tradition that predated the influence of [Nestorius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nestorius), the origin of which might lie in certain sections of the [School of Antioch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_of_Antioch) or via Nestorius' teachers [Theodore of Mopsuestia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theodore_of_Mopsuestia) or [Diodore of Tarsus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diodore_of_Tarsus). The modern incarnation of the "[Nestorian Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nestorian_Church)" is commonly referred to as "the Assyrian Church" or fully as the [Assyrian Church of the East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrian_Church_of_the_East).

The church in Egypt ([Patriarchate of Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriarchate_of_Alexandria)) split into two groups following the [Council of Chalcedon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Chalcedon) (451), over a dispute about the relation between the divine and human natures of [Jesus](1095706.md). Eventually this led to each group [anathematising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anathema#Religious_usage) the other. Those that remained in communion with the other patriarchs (by accepting the Council of Chalcedon) are known today as the [Greek Orthodox Church of Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Church_of_Alexandria), where the adjective "Greek" refers to their ties to the Greek-speaking culture of the Byzantine Empire.

Those who disagreed with the findings of the Council of Chalcedon were the majority in Egypt. Today, they are known as the [Coptic Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coptic_Orthodox_Church), having maintained a separate patriarchate. The Coptic Orthodox Church is currently the largest Christian church in Egypt and the entire [Middle East](19323.md). Likewise, in the [Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levant) and [Mesopotamia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesopotamia), another schism occurred to the [Patriarchate of Antioch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriarchate_of_Antioch), resulting in the establishment of two parallel patriarchates which endure today: the [Syriac Orthodox Church of Antioch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syriac_Orthodox_Church) and the [Greek Orthodox Church of Antioch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Church_of_Antioch).[^cite_note-100]

Those who disagreed with the Council of Chalcedon are called *[Oriental Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriental_Orthodoxy)* to distinguish them from the *[Eastern Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox)*, who accepted the Council of Chalcedon. Oriental Orthodox are also sometimes referred to as "non-Chalcedonians" or "anti-Chalcedonians". The Oriental Orthodox Church denies that it is monophysite and prefers the term *[miaphysite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miaphysite)*, to denote the "united" nature of Jesus (two natures united into one composite nature) consistent with Cyril's theology: "The term union ... signifies the concurrence in one reality of those things which are understood to be united" and "the Word who is ineffably united with it in a manner beyond all description" ([Cyril of Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyril_of_Alexandria), *On the Unity of Christ*). This is also defined in the [Coptic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coptic_Orthodox_Church) liturgy, where it is mentioned "He made it \[his humanity] one with his divinity without mingling, without confusion and without alteration", and "His divinity parted not from his humanity for a single moment nor a twinkling of an eye."[^cite_note-101] They do not accept the teachings of [Eutyches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eutyches), or [Eutychianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eutychianism).[^cite_note-102][^cite_note-103] Both the Eastern Orthodox and Oriental Orthodox churches formally believe themselves to be the continuation of the true church.

### Conversion of the South and East Slavs

Further information: [Christianisation of Bulgaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianisation_of_Bulgaria) and [Christianisation of Kievan Rus'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianisation_of_Kievan_Rus')



Miniature of [Saints Cyril and Methodius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saints_Cyril_and_Methodius), missionaries to the Slavs, from the [Menologion of Basil II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Menologion_of_Basil_II), c. 11th century

In the ninth and tenth centuries, Christianity made great inroads into pagan Europe, including [Bulgaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Bulgarian_Empire) (864) and later [Kievan Rus'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kievan_Rus') (988). This work was made possible by [Cyril and Methodius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyril_and_Methodius) of [Thessaloniki](40471.md), two brothers chosen by [Byzantine emperor Michael III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_emperor_Michael_III) to fulfil the request of [Rastislav of Moravia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rastislav_of_Moravia) for teachers who could minister to the Moravians in their own language. Cyril and Methodius began translating the [divine liturgy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divine_liturgy), other liturgical texts, and the [Gospels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospels) along with some other [scriptural texts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bible) into local languages; with time, as these translations were copied by speakers of other dialects, the hybrid literary language [Church Slavonic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_Slavonic) was created. Originally sent to convert the Slavs of [Great Moravia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Moravia), Cyril and Methodius were forced to compete with [Frankish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frankish_mythology) missionaries from the Roman diocese; their [disciples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Sava_\(disciple_of_Saints_Cyril_and_Methodius\)) were driven out of Great Moravia in AD 886 and emigrated to [Bulgaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Bulgarian_Empire).[^cite_note-104]

After the [Christianisation of Bulgaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianisation_of_Bulgaria) in 864, [the disciples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Angelar) of [Cyril and Methodius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyril_and_Methodius) in [Bulgaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Bulgarian_Empire), the most important being [Clement of Ohrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clement_of_Ohrid) and [Naum of Preslav](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naum_of_Preslav), were of great importance to the Orthodox faith in the [First Bulgarian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Bulgarian_Empire). In a short time they managed to prepare and instruct the future [Bulgarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Bulgarian_Empire) clergy into the biblical texts and in AD 870 the [Fourth Council of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourth_Council_of_Constantinople_\(Roman_Catholic\)) granted the Bulgarians the oldest organised [autocephalous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autocephalous) Slavic Orthodox Church, which shortly thereafter became Patriarchate. The success of the [conversion of the Bulgarians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianisation_of_Bulgaria) facilitated the conversion of the [East Slavs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Slavs).[^cite_note-105] A major event in this effort was the development of the [Cyrillic script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyrillic_script) in [Bulgaria](3415.md), at the [Preslav Literary School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preslav_Literary_School) in the ninth century; this script, along with the liturgical [Old Church Slavonic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Church_Slavonic), also called [Old Bulgarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Bulgarian), was declared official in [Bulgaria](3415.md) in 893.[^cite_note-106][^cite_note-107][^cite_note-108]

The work of Cyril and Methodius and their disciples had a major impact on the [Serbs](59512.md) as well.[^cite_note-Cyril-109][^cite_note-110][^cite_note-111][^cite_note-112][^cite_note-113][^cite_note-114][^cite_note-115][^cite_note-116][^cite_note-117] They accepted Christianity collectively along familial and tribal lines, a gradual process that occurred between the seventh and ninth centuries. In commemoration of their baptisms, each Serbian family or tribe began to celebrate an exclusively Serbian custom called [Slava (patron saint)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slava_\(patron_saint\)) in a special way to honour the saint on whose day they received the sacrament of [baptism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptism). It is the most solemn day of the year for all Serbs of the Orthodox faith and has played a role of vital importance in the history of the Serbian people. Slava remains a celebration of the conversion of the Serbian people, which the church blessed and proclaimed a church institution.[^cite_note-118]

The missionaries to the East and [South Slavs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Slavs) had great success in part because they used the people's native language rather than [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language), the predominant language of the Byzantine Empire, or [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin), as the Roman priests did.[^cite_note-Cyril-109] Perhaps the greatest legacy of their efforts is the Russian Orthodox Church, which is the largest of the Orthodox churches.[^cite_note-119]

### Great Schism (1054)

Main article: [East–West Schism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East%E2%80%93West_Schism)

In the 11th century, what was recognised as the [Great Schism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East%E2%80%93West_Schism) took place between [Rome](25458.md) and [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople), which led to separation between the Church of the West, the Catholic Church, and the Eastern Byzantine churches, now the Orthodox.[^cite_note-NatGeo-120] There were doctrinal issues like the [filioque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filioque) clause and the authority of the [Pope](23056.md) involved in the split, but these were greatly exacerbated by political factors of both Church and state, and by cultural and linguistic differences between Latins and Greeks. Regarding [papal supremacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papal_supremacy), the Eastern half grew disillusioned with the Pope's centralisation of power, as well as his blatant attempts of excluding the Eastern half in regard to papal approvals. It had previously been the case that the emperor would have a say when a new Pope was elected, but towards the high Middle Ages, the Christians in Rome were slowly consolidating power and removing Byzantine influence. However, even before this exclusionary tendency from the West, well before 1054, the Eastern and Western halves of the Church were in perpetual conflict, particularly during the periods of Eastern [iconoclasm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iconoclasm) and the [Photian schism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Photian_schism).[^cite_note-121]



Latin Crusaders [sacking the city of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Constantinople_\(1204\)), the capital of the Eastern Orthodox–controlled [Byzantine Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire), in 1204

The final breach is often considered to have arisen after the capture and sacking of Constantinople by the [Fourth Crusade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourth_Crusade) in 1204; the final break with Rome occurred circa 1450. The sacking of [Church of Holy Wisdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_Holy_Wisdom) and establishment of the [Latin Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_Empire) as a seeming attempt to supplant the Orthodox [Byzantine Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire) in 1204 is viewed with some rancour to the present day. In 2004, [Pope John Paul II](23805.md) extended a formal apology for the sacking of Constantinople in 1204, which had also been strongly condemned by the Pope at the time, [Innocent III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Innocent_III); the apology was formally accepted by [Patriarch Bartholomew of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriarch_Bartholomew_of_Constantinople). However, many items stolen during this time, such as [holy relics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_relics) and riches, are still held in various European cities, particularly [Venice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venice).[^cite_note-122][^cite_note-123]

Reunion was attempted twice, at the 1274 [Second Council of Lyon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Council_of_Lyon) and the 1439 [Council of Florence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Florence). The Council of Florence briefly reestablished communion between East and West, which lasted until after the fall of Constantinople in 1453. In each case, however, the councils were rejected by the Orthodox people as a whole, and the union of Florence also became very politically difficult after Constantinople came under Ottoman rule. However, in the time since, several local Orthodox Christian churches have renewed union with Rome, known as the [Eastern Catholic Churches](214903.md). Recent decades have seen a renewal of ecumenical spirit and dialogue between the Catholic and Orthodox churches.[^cite_note-124]

### Greek Church under Ottoman rule

Main articles: [Christianity in the Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_the_Ottoman_Empire) and [History of the Eastern Orthodox Church under the Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Eastern_Orthodox_Church_under_the_Ottoman_Empire)

The Byzantine Empire never fully recovered from the sack of Constantinople in 1204. Over the next two centuries, it entered a precipitous decline in both territory and influence. In 1453, a much-diminished Byzantine Empire fell to the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md), ending what was once the most powerful state in the Orthodox Christian world, if not in all [Christendom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christendom). By this time [Egypt](8087628.md), another major centre of Eastern Christianity, had been under [Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim) control for some seven centuries; most Eastern Orthodox communities [across southeastern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_conquest_of_the_Balkans) gradually came under Ottoman rule by the 16th century.



Greek Orthodox massacred during the [Greek genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_genocide) in Smyrna in 1922

Under the Ottomans, the [Greek Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Church_of_Constantinople) acquired substantial power as an autonomous *[millet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millet_\(Ottoman_Empire\))*. The ecumenical patriarch was the religious and administrative ruler of the *[Rûm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R%C3%BBm)*, an Ottoman administrative unit meaning "Roman", which encompassed all [Orthodox subjects of the Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rum_Millet) regardless of ethnicity. While legally subordinate to Muslims and [subject to various restrictions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhimmi), the Orthodox community was generally tolerated until the rise of nationalist movements in the late 19th and early 20th centuries, and left to govern its own internal affairs, both religiously and legally. Until the empire's dissolution in the early 20th century, Orthodox Christians would remain the largest non-Muslim minority, and at times among the wealthiest and most politically influential.

During the period 1914–1923 in Asia Minor (Anatolia) the [Greek genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_genocide) took place by the Ottomans. During the Greek genocide, many Orthodox Christians were persecuted and killed. The culmination of the martyrdom was the [Asia Minor Catastrophe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asia_Minor_Catastrophe) with the killing of a large number of Orthodox. Among them, 347 clergymen of the Smyrna region and [Metropolitan of Smyrna Chrysostomos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chrysostomos_of_Smyrna) were tortured and killed. The period 1923–1924 was followed by the obligatory [population exchange between Greece and Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_exchange_between_Greece_and_Turkey).[^cite_note-125]

### Russian Orthodox Church in the Russian Empire



[Uspenski Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uspenski_Cathedral), a main cathedral of the [Finnish Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_Orthodox_Church) in [Helsinki, Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helsinki,_Finland), was built [under Imperial Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Duchy_of_Finland).

By the time most Orthodox communities came under Muslim rule in the mid-15th century, Orthodoxy was very strong in Russia, which had maintained close cultural and political ties with the Byzantine Empire; roughly two decades after the fall of Constantinople, [Ivan III of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_III_of_Russia) married [Sophia Palaiologina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sophia_Palaiologina), a niece of the last Byzantine Emperor [Constantine XI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantine_XI), and styled himself Tsar ("Caesar") or *imperator*. In 1547, his grandson [Ivan IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_IV), a devout Orthodox Christian, cemented the title as "Tsar of All Rus", establishing Russia's first centralised state with divinely appointed rulers. In 1589, the Patriarchate of Constantinople granted autocephalous status to Moscow, the capital of what was now the largest Orthodox Christian polity; the city thereafter referred to itself as the [Third Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Rome#Muscovy_and_Imperial_Russia)—the cultural and religious heir of Constantinople.

Until 1666, when Patriarch Nikon was deposed by the [tsar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_tsar), the [Russian Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church) had been independent of the State.[^cite_note-126] In 1721, the first Russian Emperor, [Peter I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_I_of_Russia), abolished completely the patriarchate and effectively made the church a department of the government, ruled by a [most holy synod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Most_Holy_Synod) composed of senior bishops and lay bureaucrats appointed by the Emperor himself. Over time, Imperial Russia would style itself a protector and patron of all Orthodox Christians, especially those within the Ottoman Empire.[^cite_note-127]

For nearly 200 years, until the [Bolsheviks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolshevik)' [October Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/October_Revolution) of 1917, the Russian Orthodox Church remained, in effect, a governmental agency and an instrument of tsarist rule. It was used to varying degrees in imperial campaigns of [Russification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russification), and was even allowed to levy [taxes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tax) on [peasants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peasant). The church's close ties with the state came to a head under Nicholas I (1825–1855), who explicitly made Orthodoxy a [core doctrine of imperial unity and legitimacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodoxy,_Autocracy,_and_Nationality). The Orthodox faith became further tied to Russian identity and nationalism, while the church was further subordinated to the interests of the state. Consequently, Russian Orthodox Church, along with the imperial regime to which it belonged, came to be presented as an [enemy of the people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enemy_of_the_people) by the [Bolsheviks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolsheviks) and other Russian revolutionaries.[^cite_note-128]

### Eastern Orthodox churches under Communist rule

See also: [Persecution of Christians in the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Christians_in_the_Soviet_Union)

A church being dynamited

1931 demolition of the [Cathedral of Christ the Saviour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathedral_of_Christ_the_Saviour) in [Moscow](19004.md)

Large church

The rebuilt Cathedral of Christ the Saviour, currently the third-tallest Orthodox church

After the [October Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/October_Revolution) of 1917, part of the clergy of the Russian Orthodox Church fled abroad to escape [Bolshevik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolshevik) persecutions, founding an [independent church in exile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church_Outside_Russia), which reunified with its Russian counterpart in 2007.[^cite_note-129] Others formed the present-day [Orthodox Church in America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Church_in_America),[^cite_note-130] and the [American Orthodox Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Orthodox_Catholic_Church) (the latter whose bishops were considered *[episcopi vagantes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Episcopi_vagantes)*, yet also accepted back into mainstream Orthodoxy).[^cite_note-131][^cite_note-\:22-132][^cite_note-\:1-133][^cite_note-134][^cite_note-Anson2006-135] Some actions against Orthodox priests and believers along with [execution](5902.md) included [torture](47702.md), being sent to [prison camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulag), [labour camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sharashka) or [mental hospitals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psikhushka).[^cite_note-136][^cite_note-137] In the first five years after the Bolshevik revolution, 28 bishops and 1,200 priests were executed.[^cite_note-time-138]

After Nazi Germany's attack on the Soviet Union in 1941, [Joseph Stalin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Stalin) revived the Russian Orthodox Church to intensify patriotic support for the war effort. By 1957 about 22,000 Russian Orthodox churches had become active. However, in 1959, [Nikita Khrushchev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikita_Khrushchev) initiated his own campaign against the Russian Orthodox Church and forced the closure of about 12,000 churches. It is estimated that 50,000 clergy had been executed between the revolution and the end of the Khrushchev era. Members of the church hierarchy were jailed or forced out, their places taken by docile clergy, many of whom had ties with the KGB. By 1985 fewer than 7,000 churches remained active.[^cite_note-time-138]

[Albania](738.md) was the only state to have declared itself [officially fully atheist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_atheism).[^cite_note-Elsie_p27-139] In some other Communist states such as Romania, the [Romanian Orthodox Church](384458.md) as an organisation enjoyed relative freedom and even prospered, albeit under strict secret police control. That, however, did not rule out demolishing churches and monasteries as part of broader [systematisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Systematisation) (urban planning), and state persecution of individual believers. As an example of the latter, Romania stands out as a country which ran a [specialised institution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pite%C8%99ti_Prison) where many Orthodox (along with people of other faiths) were subjected to [psychological punishment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychological_punishment) or torture and [mind control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brainwashing) experimentation in order to force them give up their religious convictions. However, this was only supported by one faction within the regime, and lasted only three years. The Communist authorities closed down the prison in 1952, and punished many of those responsible for abuses (twenty of them were sentenced to death).[^cite_note-140][^cite_note-141]

### Post-communism to 21st century

Since the [dissolution of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union), and the subsequent [Fall of Communist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Communist) governments across the [Orthodox world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_world), there has been marked growth in Christian Orthodoxy, particularly in Russia. According to the [Pew Research Religion & Public Life Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Religion_&_Public_Life_Project), between 1991 and 2008, the share of Russian adults identifying as Orthodox Christian rose from 31 per cent to 72 per cent, based on analysis of three waves of data (1991, 1998 and 2008) from the [International Social Survey Programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Social_Survey_Programme) (ISSP), a collaborative effort involving social scientists in about 50 countries.[^cite_note-142]

Pew research conducted in 2017 found a doubling in the global Orthodox population since the early 20th century, with the greatest resurgence in Russia.[^cite_note-143] In the former Soviet Union—where the largest Orthodox communities live—self-identified Orthodox Christians generally report low levels of observance and piety: In Russia, only 6% of Orthodox Christian adults reported attending church at least weekly, 15% say religion is "very important" in their lives, and 18% say they pray daily; other former Soviet republics display similarly low levels of religious observance.[^cite_note-ReferenceA-144]

#### Moscow–Constantinople schisms

##### 1996

Main article: [1996 Moscow–Constantinople schism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1996_Moscow%E2%80%93Constantinople_schism)

Since 1923, the [Orthodox Church of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Church_of_Estonia) separated from the [Russian Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church) due to the imprisonment of [Patriarch Tikhon of Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriarch_Tikhon_of_Moscow), and the church in the [Republic of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Estonia) falling out of communication with the Russian Church. They petitioned to be placed under direct control of the [Ecumenical Patriarch of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecumenical_Patriarch_of_Constantinople), operating as an autonomous church. In 1944 the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) annexed Estonia and outlawed the Orthodox Church of Estonia, forcefully bringing their churches back under the control of the Moscow Patriarch. However, the church's Primate, Metropolitan Aleksander, fled to [Sweden](5058739.md) with 21 clergymen and 8,000 followers and established a synod there operating there throughout the [Cold War](325329.md).[^cite_note-Toom-145]

In 1993, the synod of the Orthodox Church of Estonia in Exile was re-registered and on 20 February 1996, [Bartholomew I of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bartholomew_I_of_Constantinople) restored the church's position as subordinate to Constantinople, not Moscow. [Patriarch Alexy II of Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriarch_Alexy_II_of_Moscow), who had been born in Estonia, rejected this loss of territory, and severed ties with Patriarch Bartholomew on February 23, removing his name from the diptychs. The two sides would then negotiate in [Zürich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Z%C3%BCrich), and a settlement was reached on 16 May 1996. In it, the ethnically Estonian population of Estonia would be under the jurisdiction of the [Estonian Apostolic Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Apostolic_Orthodox_Church), while the ethnically Russian population of Estonia would be under the jurisdiction of the [Estonian Orthodox Church of the Moscow Patriarchate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Orthodox_Church_of_the_Moscow_Patriarchate). After signing the document the Russian Church restored communion with the Ecumenical Patriarchate.[^cite_note-146][^cite_note-HD-147]

##### 2018

Main article: [2018 Moscow–Constantinople schism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Moscow%E2%80%93Constantinople_schism)



Patriarch Bartholomew signing the [tomos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tomos_\(Eastern_Orthodox_Church\)) of the [autocephaly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autocephaly) of the [OCU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Church_of_Ukraine)

Since the [Baptism of Rus'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptism_of_Rus')[^cite_note-148] in 867 the Orthodox church in Ukraine was led by the [Metropolitan of Kiev and all Rus'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_of_Kiev_and_all_Rus') who was subordinate to the [Ecumenical Patriarch of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecumenical_Patriarch_of_Constantinople) and was largely governed by the [Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish%E2%80%93Lithuanian_Commonwealth) since the territory was conquered in the [Galicia–Volhynia Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galicia%E2%80%93Volhynia_Wars), ending in 1392. Poland-Lithuania lost the territory to Russia as part of the peace deal of the [Great Northern War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Polish_War_\(1654%E2%80%931667\)) in 1654. In 1686 [Dionysius IV of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dionysius_IV_of_Constantinople) transferred the territory to the [Patriarch of Moscow and all Rus'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriarch_of_Moscow_and_all_Rus'). In 1924, Orthodox churches in Ukraine besides the Metropolitan of Kyiv were placed under the jurisdiction of the [Polish Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_Orthodox_Church) by the Ecumenical Patriarch as an autonomous church, however, the Russian Church never agreed to nor recognised this transfer, mostly due to [Patriarch Tikhon of Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriarch_Tikhon_of_Moscow) and most of the Russian Church's leaders being imprisoned by Soviet officials.[^cite_note-149][^cite_note-150][^cite_note-151] The [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union), initially, had a policy of repression against the Orthodox Church, regardless of its denomination. However, after the start of the [Nazi Invasion of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Barbarossa), [Joseph Stalin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Stalin) transformed the Russian Church into a propaganda tool to intensify patriotic support for the war effort. Following Soviet victory in the war, various autonomous and Independent Orthodox churches around eastern Europe were forcefully integrated or reintegrated into the Russian Church, including the church in Ukraine. Many of the church's leaders at this time were installed and closely monitored by the [NKVD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NKVD) to ensure the church's support for the Soviet Union.[^cite_note-152][^cite_note-153][^cite_note-154]

This situation led to the rise of rival, anti-Russian and anti-Soviet churches within Ukraine, including the [Ukrainian Autocephalous Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_Autocephalous_Orthodox_Church) (UAOC), founded in 1917 which declared itself the restored autonomous church that existed prior to 1686 but had been eradicated within Soviet Ukraine by the 1930s. The church was largely supported by Ukrainian émigrés and diaspora, and was restored as a legally recognised church by the Ukrainian government in 1991.[^cite_note-155] In 1992, the [Ukrainian Orthodox Church – Kyiv Patriarchate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_Orthodox_Church_%E2%80%93_Kyiv_Patriarchate) (UOC-KP) came into existence, being founded by members of the Russian Church [defrocked](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defrocked) for insubordination, alongside support with the Ukrainian émigré community. The church submitted a request for Ukrainian autocephaly at its founding synod in Kyiv in 1992.[^cite_note-156][^cite_note-157] These churches were competing with the [Ukrainian Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_Orthodox_Church_\(Moscow_Patriarchate\)) (UOC-MP), the Russian Church in Ukraine.[^cite_note-158][^cite_note-159]

On 11 October 2018, the [Holy Synod of the Ecumenical Patriarchate of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Synod_of_the_Ecumenical_Patriarchate_of_Constantinople) revoked the Russian Church's letter of issue, allowing them to ordain the Metropolitan of Kyiv, re-established a [stauropegion](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/stauropegion) in Kyiv, and lifted the Russian Church's excommunication of members of the UAOC and the UOC-KP. In response, on 15 October, the [Holy Synod of the Russian Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Synod_of_the_Russian_Orthodox_Church) severed all ties with the [Ecumenical Patriarchate of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecumenical_Patriarchate_of_Constantinople) and barred all members of the Russian Church from receiving communion or sacraments from any churches with ties to the Ecumenical Patriarchate.[^cite_note-160][^cite_note-161] On 15 December 2018, the UAOC and UOC-KP voted to merge in the [Unification council of the Eastern Orthodox churches of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unification_council_of_the_Eastern_Orthodox_churches_of_Ukraine), forming the restored [Orthodox Church of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Church_of_Ukraine), with [Epiphanius of Kyiv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epiphanius_of_Kyiv), of the UOC-KP, becoming the first primate of the unified church.[^cite_note-162] On 5 January 2019, Bartholomew I signed the official [tomos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tomos_\(Eastern_Orthodox_Church\)) that granted [autocephaly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autocephaly) to the Orthodox Church of Ukraine.[^cite_note-163]

In addition to severing ties with the Ecumenical Patriarchate, the Russian Church has also severed communion with Archbishop [Ieronymos II of Athens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ieronymos_II_of_Athens) primate of the [Church of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_Greece),[^cite_note-164][^cite_note-165][^cite_note-166] [Patriarch Theodore II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriarch_Theodore_II_of_Alexandria) [of Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Patriarchate_of_Alexandria),[^cite_note-167][^cite_note-168][^cite_note-169][^cite_note-170] and Archbishop [Chrysostomos II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chrysostomos_II_of_Cyprus) of [Cyprus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_Cyprus).[^cite_note-171][^cite_note-172][^cite_note-173][^cite_note-174] In response to the severing of ties with the Ecumenical Patriarchate, the [Archdiocese of Russian Orthodox Churches in Western Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archdiocese_of_Russian_Orthodox_Churches_in_Western_Europe) (AROCWE), voted to dissolve itself, although the vote failed, it resulted in a split in AROCWE, with several churches leaving to form the "Vicariate of Russian Tradition of the Metropolis of France", while [John (Renneteau)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_\(Renneteau\)?action=edit\&redlink=1), head of the AROCWE, personally joined the Russian Church.[^cite_note-175][^cite_note-176][^cite_note-177][^cite_note-178][^cite_note-179] After the [2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine](79400268.md), the UOC-MP severed all ties with the Russian Church.[^cite_note-180][^cite_note-181][^cite_note-182]

## Organisation and leadership

Main article: [Eastern Orthodox Church organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church_organization)

See also: [Canon law of the Eastern Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canon_law_of_the_Eastern_Orthodox_Church)



A timeline showing the main autocephalous Eastern Orthodox churches, from an Eastern Orthodox point of view, up to 2022



The [canonical territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canonical_territory) of the main autocephalous and autonomous Eastern Orthodox jurisdictions as of 2022



[St. Nicholas Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Nicholas_Cathedral_\(Seoul\)) in [Seoul](19159283.md), Korea

The Eastern Orthodox Church is a fellowship of [autocephalous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autocephalous) ([Ancient Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek_language): αὐτοκέφαλος; "self-headed") churches, with the [ecumenical patriarch of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecumenical_patriarch_of_Constantinople) recognised as having *[primus inter pares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primus_inter_pares)* status. The patriarch of Constantinople has the honour of primacy, but his title is only *first among equals* and he has no real authority over churches other than the Constantinopolitan church. Rather, his role is limited to defined prerogatives interpreted by the ecumenical patriarch.[^cite_note-bbc-183][^cite_note-184][^cite_note-185] At times, though, the office of the ecumenical patriarch has been accused of Constantinopolitan or Eastern [papism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papism).[^cite_note-186][^cite_note-187][^cite_note-188]

The Eastern Orthodox Church considers [Jesus Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_Christ) to be the head of the Church and the Church to be his body.
