It is believed that Church authority and the [grace of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grace_of_God) is directly passed down to Orthodox [bishops](4092.md) and other [clergy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clergy) through the [laying on of hands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laying_on_of_hands)—a practice started by the [New Testament apostles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Testament_apostles)—and that this unbroken historical link is an essential element of the [true church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_true_church) ([Acts 8\:17](https://bible.oremus.org/?passage=Acts%208\:17\&version=nrsv); [1 Timothy 4\:14](https://bible.oremus.org/?passage=1%20Timothy%204\:14\&version=nrsv); [Hebrews 6\:2](https://bible.oremus.org/?passage=Hebrews%206\:2\&version=nrsv)) The Eastern Orthodox Church asserts that [apostolic succession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostolic_succession) requires apostolic faith, and bishops without apostolic faith, who are in [heresy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heresy), forfeit their claim to apostolic succession.[^cite_note-189]

The Eastern Orthodox communion is organised into several regional churches, which are either autocephalous or lower-ranking [autonomous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomy_\(Eastern_Orthodoxy\)) ("self-governing") church bodies unified in [theology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theology) and worship. These include the fourteen autocephalous churches of the [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecumenical_Patriarchate_of_Constantinople), [Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Church_of_Alexandria), [Antioch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Patriarchate_of_Antioch_and_All_the_East), [Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Patriarchate_of_Jerusalem), [Georgia](433340.md), [Cyprus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_Cyprus), [Bulgaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgarian_Orthodox_Church), [Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_Orthodox_Church), [Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church), [Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_Greece), [Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_Orthodox_Church), [Romania](384458.md), [Albania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albanian_Orthodox_Church), and the [Czech Republic and Slovakia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Czech_and_Slovak_Orthodox_Church), which were officially invited to the [Pan-Orthodox Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Orthodox_Council) of 2016;[^cite_note-190] the [Orthodox Church in America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Church_in_America) formed in 1970; the autocephalous [Orthodox Church of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Church_of_Ukraine) created in 2019; the [Macedonian Orthodox Church – Ohrid Archbishopric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macedonian_Orthodox_Church_%E2%80%93_Ohrid_Archbishopric), granted autocephaly by the Serbian Orthodox Church in 2022;[^cite_note-191] and a number of autonomous churches.[^cite_note-bbc-183] Each church has a ruling [bishop](4092.md) and a [holy synod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Synod) to administer its jurisdiction and lead the Eastern Orthodox Church in the preservation and teaching of the apostolic and [patristic](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/patristic) traditions and church practices.

Each bishop has a territory ([see](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Episcopal_see)) over which he governs. His main duty is to make sure the traditions and practices of the Eastern Orthodox Church are preserved. Bishops are equal in authority and cannot interfere in the jurisdiction of another bishop. Administratively, these bishops and their territories are organised into various autocephalous groups or [synods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synod) of bishops who gather together at least twice each year to discuss the state of affairs within their respective sees. While bishops and their autocephalous synods have the ability to administer guidance in individual cases, their actions do not usually set precedents that affect the entire Eastern Orthodox Church. Bishops are almost always chosen from the monastic ranks and must remain unmarried.

### Church councils



Oldest extant manuscript of the [Nicene Creed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicene_Creed), dated to the fifth century

The ecumenical councils followed a democratic form, with each bishop having one vote. Though present and allowed to speak before the council, members of the [Imperial Roman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_Roman)/[Byzantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine) court, abbots, priests, deacons, monks and laymen were not allowed to vote. The primary goal of these great synods was to verify and confirm the fundamental beliefs of the [Great Christian Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Church) as truth, and to remove as heresy any false teachings that would threaten the Christian Church. The [pope of Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_of_Rome) at that time held the position of *[primus inter pares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primus_inter_pares)* ("first among equals") and, while he was not present at any of the councils, he continued to hold this title until the [East–West Schism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East%E2%80%93West_Schism) of 1054.[^cite_note-192][^cite_note-193][^cite_note-194][^cite_note-195]

Other councils have helped to define the Eastern Orthodox position, specifically the [Quinisext Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quinisext_Council), the Synods of [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople), [879–880](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourth_Council_of_Constantinople_\(Eastern_Orthodox\)), [1341, 1347, 1351](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fifth_Council_of_Constantinople), 1583, 1819, and [1872](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phyletism), the [Synod of Iași](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synod_of_Ia%C8%99i), 1642, and the Pan-Orthodox [Synod of Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synod_of_Jerusalem_\(1672\)), 1672; the [Pan-Orthodox Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Orthodox_Council), held in Greece in 2016, was the only such Eastern Orthodox council in modern times.

According to Eastern Orthodox teaching the position of "first among equals" gives no additional power or authority to the bishop that holds it, but rather that this person sits as organisational head of a council of equals (like a president).[^cite_note-196]

One of the decisions made by the [First Council of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Council_of_Constantinople) (the second ecumenical council, meeting in 381) and supported by later such councils was that the Patriarch of Constantinople should be given equal honour to the Pope of Rome since Constantinople was considered to be the "[New Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Rome)". According to the third [canon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canon_\(canon_law\)) of the second ecumenical council: "Because \[Constantinople] is new Rome, the bishop of Constantinople is to enjoy the privileges of honor after the bishop of Rome".[^cite_note-197]

The 28th canon of the fourth ecumenical council clarified this point by stating: "For the Fathers rightly granted privileges to the throne of Old Rome because it was the royal city. And the One Hundred and Fifty most religious Bishops (i.e. the second ecumenical council in 381) actuated by the same consideration, gave equal privileges to the most holy throne of New Rome, justly judging that the city which is honoured with the Sovereignty and the Senate, and enjoys equal privileges with the old imperial Rome, should in ecclesiastical matters also be magnified as she is."[^cite_note-198]

Because of the schism, the Eastern Orthodox no longer recognise the primacy of the pope of Rome. The patriarch of Constantinople therefore, like the Pope before him, now enjoys the title of "first among equals".

### Adherents

Further information: [Eastern Orthodoxy by country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodoxy_by_country)



Distribution of Eastern Orthodoxy in the world by country&#x20;

&#x20; Dominant religion

&#x20; Important minority religion

The most reliable estimates currently available number Eastern Orthodox adherents at around 220 million,[^cite_note-199][^cite_note-200][^cite_note-201][^cite_note-Peworthodox-202][^cite_note-CSGC-2019-203] making Eastern Orthodoxy the second largest Christian [communion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koinonia) in the world after the [Catholic Church](606848.md).[^cite_note-Adherents3-14][^cite_note-205]

According to the 2015 Yearbook of International Religious Demography, as of 2010, the Eastern Orthodox population was 4% of the global population, declining from 7.1% in 1910. The study also found a decrease in proportional terms, with Eastern Orthodox Christians making up 12.2% of the world's total Christian population in 2015 compared to 20.4% a century earlier.[^cite_note-Yearbook-206] A 2017 report by the [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center) reached similar figures, noting that Eastern Orthodoxy has seen slower growth and less geographic spread than Catholicism and Protestantism, which were driven by [missionary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missionary) activity across the world.[^cite_note-207]

Over two-thirds of all Eastern Orthodox members are concentrated in [Southeast Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeast_Europe), [Eastern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Europe) and [Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Russia),[^cite_note-208] with significant minorities in [Central Asia](6742.md) and the [Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levant). However, Eastern Orthodoxy has become more globalised over the last century, seeing greater growth in [Western Europe](33800.md), [the Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_World), and parts of Africa; churches are present in the major cities of most countries.[^cite_note-209] Adherents constitute the largest single religious [community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faith) in Russia[^cite_note-Sreda2012-210][^cite_note-213]—which is home to roughly half the world's Eastern Orthodox Christians—and are the majority in [Ukraine](31750.md),[^cite_note-CIAWeb-212][^cite_note-Razumkov2006-214] [Romania](25445.md),[^cite_note-CIAWeb-212] [Belarus](3457.md),[^cite_note-Belarus2011-215] [Greece](12108.md),[^cite_note-216][^cite_note-CIAWeb-212] [Serbia](29265.md),[^cite_note-CIAWeb-212] [Bulgaria](3415.md),[^cite_note-CIAWeb-212] [Moldova](19260.md),[^cite_note-CIAWeb-212] [Georgia](48768.md),[^cite_note-CIAWeb-212] [North Macedonia](23564616.md),[^cite_note-CIAWeb-212] [Cyprus](5593.md),[^cite_note-CIAWeb-212] and [Montenegro](20760.md);[^cite_note-CIAWeb-212] communities also dominate the disputed territories of [Abkhazia](18933375.md), [South Ossetia](318381.md) and [Transnistria](376581.md). Significant Eastern Orthodox minorities exist in [Bosnia and Herzegovina](3463.md),[^cite_note-217][^cite_note-CIAWeb-212] [Latvia](17514.md),[^cite_note-Tieslietu-218] [Estonia](28222445.md),[^cite_note-219] [Kazakhstan](16642.md),[^cite_note-220] [Kyrgyzstan](170131.md),[^cite_note-221] [Lebanon](17771.md)[^cite_note-freedom-222] and [Syria](7515849.md).[^cite_note-CIAWeb-212]

Eastern Orthodox Christianity is a growing religion in certain Western countries, primarily through labour migration, from Eastern Europe, and to a much lesser degree conversion.[^cite_note-Hämmerli-223] Ireland saw a doubling of its Eastern Orthodox population between 2006 and 2011.[^cite_note-Hämmerli-223][^cite_note-224][^cite_note-225] Spain and Germany have the [largest communities in Western Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodoxy_by_country), at roughly 1.5 million each,[^cite_note-226][^cite_note-227] followed by Italy with around 900,000 and France with between 500,000 and 700,000.

In the [Americas](29833.md), four countries have over 100,000 Eastern Orthodox Christians: Canada, Mexico, Brazil, and the United States; all but the latter had fewer than 20,000 at the turn of the 20th century.[^cite_note-pewforum.org-228] The U.S. has seen its community more than quadruple since 1910, from 460,000 to 1.8 million as of 2017;[^cite_note-pewforum.org-228] consequently, the number of Eastern Orthodox parishes has been growing, with a 16% increase between 2000 and 2010.[^cite_note-Jones2010-229][^cite_note-231][^cite_note-234]

Turkey, which for centuries once had one of the largest Eastern Orthodox communities, saw its overall Christian population fall from roughly one-fifth in 1914 to 2.5% in 1927.[^cite_note-235] This was predominantly due to the [dissolution of the Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Ottoman_Empire), which saw most Christian territories become independent nations. The remaining Christian population was reduced further by large-scale genocides against the [Armenian](140376.md), [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_genocide), [Assyrian communities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrian_genocide); subsequent [population exchanges between Greece and Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_exchanges_between_Greece_and_Turkey)[^cite_note-236] and Bulgaria and Turkey; and associated [emigration of Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_emigration) to foreign countries (mostly in Europe and [the Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Americas)).[^cite_note-237] Today, only 0.2% of Turkey's population represent [various Christian denominations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Turkey) (320,000).[^cite_note-238][^cite_note-CIAWeb-212]
