## Theology

Main article: [Eastern Orthodox Christian theology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Christian_theology)

### Trinity



*[The Trinity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinity_\(Andrei_Rublev\))* by Russian icon painter [Andrei Rublev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrei_Rublev), early 15th century

Eastern Orthodox Christians believe in the [Trinity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinity), three distinct, divine persons (*[hypostases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypostasis_\(philosophy\))*), without overlap or [modality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabellianism) among them, who each have the same divine [essence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Essence) (*ousia*, Greek: οὐσία)—uncreated, immaterial, and [eternal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_and_eternity).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWare1993208–211-276] These three persons are typically distinguished by their relation to each other. The [Father](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_the_Father) is eternal and not begotten and does not proceed from any, the [Son](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_the_Son) is eternal and begotten of the Father, and the [Holy Spirit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Spirit_in_Christianity) is eternal and proceeds from the Father.[^cite_note-Theokritoff_2010-277] Orthodox doctrine regarding the Trinity is summarised in the [Nicene Creed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicene_Creed#Niceno-Constantinopolitan_Creed).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWare1993202-278]

Eastern Orthodox Christians believe in a [monotheistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monotheistic) [conception of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_in_Christianity) (God is only one), which is both [transcendent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transcendence_\(religion\)) (wholly independent of, and removed from, the material universe) and [immanent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immanent) (involved in the material universe).[^cite_note-Theokritoff_2010-277]

In discussing God's relationship to his creation, Eastern Orthodox theology [distinguishes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Essence%E2%80%93Energies_distinction) between God's eternal essence, which is totally transcendent, and his *uncreated energies*, which is how he reaches humanity.[^cite_note-Theokritoff_2010-277] The God who is transcendent and the God who touches mankind are one and the same.[^cite_note-Theokritoff_2010-277] That is, these energies are not something that proceed from God or that God produces, but rather they are God himself: distinct, yet inseparable from God's inner being.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWare199367–69-279] This view is often called [Palamism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palamism).

In understanding the [Trinity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinity) as "one God in three persons", "three persons" is not to be emphasised more than "one God", and vice versa. While the three persons are distinct, they are united in one divine essence, and their oneness is expressed in community and action so completely that they cannot be considered separately. For example, their salvation of mankind is an activity engaged in common: "Christ became man by the good will of the Father and by the cooperation of the Holy Spirit. Christ sends the Holy Spirit who proceeds from the Father, and the Holy Spirit forms Christ in our hearts, and thus God the Father is glorified." Their "communion of essence" is "indivisible". Trinitarian terminology—essence, hypostasis, etc.—are used "philosophically", "to answer the ideas of the heretics", and "to place the terms where they separate error and truth."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHierotheos1998128–130-280] The words do what they can do, but the nature of the Trinity in its fullness is believed to remain beyond man's comprehension and expression, a holy mystery that can only be experienced.

### Sin, salvation, and the incarnation



Crucifixion Icon from [Saint Catherine's Monastery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Catherine's_Monastery), 13th century

When Eastern Orthodox Christians refer to fallen nature they are not saying that human nature has become evil in itself. Human nature is still formed in the image of God; humans are still God's creation, and God has never created anything evil, but fallen nature remains open to evil intents and actions. It is sometimes said among Eastern Orthodox that humans are "inclined to sin"; that is, people find some sinful things attractive. It is the nature of temptation to make sinful things seem the more attractive, and it is the fallen nature of humans that seeks or succumbs to the attraction. Orthodox Christians reject the [Augustinian position](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Augustinianism) that the descendants of Adam and Eve are actually guilty of the original sin of their ancestors.[^cite_note-281]

Since the fall of man, then, it has been mankind's dilemma that no human can restore his nature to union with God's grace; it was necessary for God to effect another change in human nature. Eastern Orthodox Christians believe that Christ Jesus was both God and Man absolutely and completely, having two natures indivisibly: eternally begotten of the Father in his divinity, he was born in his humanity of a woman, Mary, by her consent, through descent of the Holy Spirit. He lived on earth, in time and history, as a man. As a man he also died, and went to the place of the dead, which is [Hades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_views_on_Hades). But being God, neither death nor Hades could contain him, and he rose to life again, in his humanity, by the power of the Holy Spirit, thus destroying the power of Hades and of death itself.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChrysostom400\[\[Paschal_Homily\]\]-282]

Through Christ's destruction of Hades' power to hold humanity hostage, he made the path to salvation effective for all the righteous who had died from the beginning of time—saving many, including Adam and Eve, who are remembered in the church as saints.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._Athanasius1982Ch._2–3,_p._318-283]

### Resurrection of Christ

The Eastern Orthodox Church understands the death and resurrection of Jesus to be real historical events, as described in the gospels of the [New Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Testament).

### Christian life



[Monastery of the Holy Trinity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monastery_of_the_Holy_Trinity,_Meteora) in Greece.

Church teaching is that Eastern Orthodox Christians, through baptism, enter a new life of salvation through repentance whose purpose is to share in the life of God through the work of the Holy Spirit. The Eastern Orthodox Christian life is a spiritual pilgrimage in which each person, through the [imitation of Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imitation_of_Christ) and *[hesychasm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hesychasm)*,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHierotheos1998234–237,_(241=Glossary)-284] cultivates the practice of unceasing prayer. Each life occurs within the life of the church as a member of the [body of Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Body_of_Christ).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGeorge2006\[httporthodoxinfocomgeneraltheosis-englishpdf_34\]-285] It is then through the fire of God's love in the action of the Holy Spirit that each member becomes more holy, more wholly unified with Christ, starting in this life and continuing in the next.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOxford_Dict_Christian_Church3rd_ed-286][^cite_note-287] The church teaches that everyone, being born in God's image, is called to [theosis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theosis_\(Eastern_Christian_theology\)), fulfilment of the image in likeness to God. God the creator, having divinity by nature, offers each person participation in divinity by cooperatively accepting His gift of grace.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGeorge2006\[httporthodoxinfocomgeneraltheosis-englishpdf_21\]-288]



*[The Vision to the Youth Bartholomew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Vision_to_the_Youth_Bartholomew)* by [Mikhail Nesterov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Nesterov) (1890).

The Eastern Orthodox Church, in understanding itself to be the [Body of Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Body_of_Christ), and similarly in understanding the Christian life to lead to the unification in Christ of all members of his body, views the church as embracing all Christ's members, those now living on earth, and also all those through the ages who have passed on to the heavenly life. "In general," Eastern Orthodox Christianity sees the Church "as a purely mystical body, the understanding of which cannot be attained through the development of a rational or [natural theology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_theology)."[^cite_note-RF-289]

The church includes the Christian saints from all times, and also judges, prophets and righteous Jews of the first covenant, Adam and Eve, even the angels and heavenly hosts.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHierotheos199825–30-290] In Eastern Orthodox services, the earthly members together with the heavenly members worship God as one community in Christ, in a union that transcends time and space and joins heaven to earth. This unity of the church is sometimes called the *communion of the saints*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHierotheos199823-291]

#### Eastern Orthodox Order of Saint Benedict

The Order of Saint Benedict is an affiliation of monastics of the Eastern Orthodox Church who strive to live according to the [Rule of St Benedict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rule_of_St_Benedict). The equivalent monastic order in the [Catholic Church](606848.md) is known as the [Order of Saint Benedict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Order_of_Saint_Benedict), abbreviated as OSB.

Within the United States, the [Antiochian Orthodox Archdiocese of North America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antiochian_Orthodox_Archdiocese_of_North_America) has at least one Benedictine monastery.[^cite_note-292]

Several Benedictine monastic houses, *[sketes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skete)* and hermitages fit within the Russian Orthodox Church outside of Russia, all of which are [*stavropegial*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stauropegic_monastery) directly under the Metropolitan. An oblate programme exists for Orthodox laity Saint Benedict Russian Orthodox Church in Oklahoma City, Oklahoma.[^cite_note-293]

### Virgin Mary and other saints



The [Theotokos of Vladimir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theotokos_of_Vladimir), one of the most venerated of Orthodox Christian icons of the [Virgin Mary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virgin_Mary)



A 17th-century Bulgarian icon depicting [Saint Elijah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Elijah) and scenes from his life

The Eastern Orthodox Church believes death and the separation of body and soul to be unnatural—a result of the [Fall of Man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Man). They also hold that the congregation of the church comprises both the living and the dead. All persons currently in heaven are considered to be [saints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint), whether their names are known or not. There are, however, those saints of distinction whom God has revealed as particularly good examples. When a saint is revealed and ultimately recognised by a large portion of the church a service of official recognition ([glorification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glorification)) is celebrated.[^cite_note-294]

This does not "make" the person a saint; it merely recognises the fact and announces it to the rest of the church. A day is prescribed for the saint's celebration, hymns composed and icons created. Numerous saints are celebrated on each day of the year. They are venerated (shown great respect and love) but not worshipped, for worship is due God alone (this view is also held by the [Oriental Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriental_Orthodoxy) and [Catholic churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_church)). In showing the saints this love and requesting their prayers, the Eastern Orthodox manifest their belief that the saints thus assist in the process of salvation for others.

Pre-eminent among the saints is the [Virgin Mary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virgin_Mary) (commonly referred to as *Theotokos* or *Bogoroditsa*: "[Mother of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mother_of_God)"). In [Eastern Orthodox theology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_theology), the Mother of God is the fulfilment of the Old Testament archetypes revealed in the [Ark of the Covenant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ark_of_the_Covenant) (because she carried the New Covenant in the person of Christ) and the [burning bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burning_bush) that appeared before [Moses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moses) (symbolising the Mother of God's carrying of God without being consumed).[^cite_note-295]

The Eastern Orthodox believe that Christ, from the moment of his conception, was both fully God and fully human. Mary is thus called the *Theotokos* or *Bogoroditsa* as an affirmation of the divinity of the one to whom she gave birth. It is also believed that her virginity was not compromised in conceiving God-incarnate, that she was not harmed and that she remained forever a virgin. Scriptural references to "brothers" of Christ are interpreted as kin, given that the word "brother" was used in multiple ways, as was the term "father". Due to her unique place in salvation history, Mary is honoured above all other saints and especially venerated for the great work that God accomplished through her.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWare1993257–258-296]

The Eastern Orthodox Church regards the bodies of all saints as holy, made such by participation in the holy mysteries, especially the communion of Christ's holy body and blood, and by the indwelling of the Holy Spirit within the church. Indeed, that persons and physical things can be made holy is a cornerstone of the doctrine of the [Incarnation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Incarnation_\(Christianity\)), made manifest also directly by God in Old Testament times through his dwelling in the Ark of the Covenant. Thus, physical items connected with saints are also regarded as holy, through their participation in the earthly works of those saints. According to church teaching and tradition, God himself bears witness to this holiness of saints' [relics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relic) through the many miracles connected with them that have been reported throughout history since biblical times, often including healing from disease and injury.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWare1993234-297]

### Eschatology

Main article: [Christian eschatology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_eschatology)



*Last Judgment*: 12th-century [Byzantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire) mosaic from [Torcello](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torcello) Cathedral

Orthodox Christians believe that when a person dies the soul is temporarily separated from the body. Though it may linger for a short period on Earth, it is ultimately escorted either to paradise ([Abraham's bosom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham's_bosom)) or the darkness of [Hades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_views_on_Hades), following the [Temporary Judgment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Particular_judgment). Orthodox do not accept the doctrine of [Purgatory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purgatory), which is held by Catholicism. The soul's experience of either of these states is only a "foretaste"—being experienced only by the soul—until the [Final Judgment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Final_Judgment), when the soul and body will be reunited.[^cite_note-Philaret-298][^cite_note-soul-299]

The Eastern Orthodox believe that the state of the soul in Hades can be affected by the love and prayers of the righteous up until the Last Judgment.[^cite_note-Philaret-298] For this reason the church offers a special [prayer for the dead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prayer_for_the_dead) on the third day, ninth day, fortieth day, and the one-year anniversary after the death of an Orthodox Christian. There are also several days throughout the year that are set aside for general commemoration of the departed, sometimes including nonbelievers. These days usually fall on a Saturday, since it was on a Saturday that Christ lay in the [Tomb](7810.md).[^cite_note-soul-299]

The Eastern Orthodox believe that after the Final Judgment:

- All souls will be reunited with their [resurrected bodies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resurrection_of_the_Dead).

- All souls will fully experience their spiritual state.

- Having been perfected, the saints will forever progress towards a deeper and fuller love of God, which equates with eternal happiness.

  [^cite_note-soul-299]

### Bible



[Alaverdi Gospels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaverdi_Gospels), an 11th-century [Georgian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_language) [illuminated manuscript](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illuminated_manuscript) [Gospel Book](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_Book)

The official Bible of the Eastern Orthodox Church contains the [Septuagint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Septuagint) text of the [Old Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Testament), with the [Book of Daniel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Daniel) given in the translation by [Theodotion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theodotion). The [Patriarchal Text](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriarchal_Text) is used for the [New Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Testament).[^cite_note-300][^cite_note-301] Orthodox Christians hold that the Bible is a verbal icon of Christ, as proclaimed by the [7th ecumenical council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Council_of_Nicaea).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWare1991209-302] They refer to the Bible as [holy scripture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_scripture), meaning writings containing the foundational truths of the Christian faith as revealed by Christ and the [Holy Spirit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Spirit_in_Christianity) to its divinely inspired human authors. Holy scripture forms the primary and authoritative written witness of [holy tradition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_tradition) and is essential as the basis for all Orthodox teaching and belief.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWare1991209_(quoting_\[\[John_Chrysostom\]\]):_"It_is_impossible_for_a_man_to_be_saved_if_he_does_not_read_the_Scriptures."-303]

Once established as holy scripture, there has never been any question that the Eastern Orthodox Church holds the full list of books to be venerable and beneficial for reading and study,[^cite_note-304] even though it informally holds some books in higher esteem than others, the four gospels highest of all. Of the subgroups significant enough to be named, the "[Anagignoskomena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anagignoskomena)" (ἀναγιγνωσκόμενα, "things that are read") comprises ten of the Old Testament books rejected in the [Protestant canon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant_canon),[^cite_note-305] but deemed by the Eastern Orthodox worthy to be read in worship services, even though they carry a lesser esteem than the 39 books of the [Hebrew canon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_Bible).[^cite_note-306] The lowest tier contains the remaining books not accepted by either Protestants or Catholics, among them, [Psalm 151](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psalm_151). Though it is a psalm, and is in the book of psalms, it is not classified as being within the Psalter (the first 150 psalms).[^cite_note-307]

In a very strict sense, it is not entirely orthodox to call the holy scripture the "Word of God". That is a title the Eastern Orthodox Church reserves for Christ, as supported in the scriptures themselves, most explicitly in the [first chapter of the Gospel of John](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_1). God's Word is not hollow, like human words. "God said, 'let there be light'; and there was light."[^cite_note-308]

The Eastern Orthodox Church does not subscribe to the Protestant doctrine of *[sola scriptura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sola_scriptura)*. The church has defined what Scripture is; it also interprets what its meaning is.[^cite_note-309] Christ promised: "When He, the Spirit of truth, has come, He will guide you into all truth".[^cite_note-310]

Scriptures are understood to contain historical fact, poetry, idiom, metaphor, simile, moral fable, parable, prophecy and [wisdom literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisdom_literature), and each bears its own consideration in its interpretation. While divinely inspired, the text still consists of words in human languages, arranged in humanly recognisable forms. The Eastern Orthodox Church does not oppose honest critical and historical study of the Bible.[^cite_note-Ware1991-311]

## Liturgy

Main articles: [Byzantine Rite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Rite), [Eastern Orthodox worship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_worship), and [Western Rite Orthodoxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Rite_Orthodoxy)



[Icon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Icon) of Ss. [Basil the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basil_the_Great) (left) and [John Chrysostom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Chrysostom), ascribed authors of the two most frequently used Eastern Orthodox Divine Liturgies, c. 1150 ([mosaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mosaic) in the [Palatine Chapel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cappella_Palatina), [Palermo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palermo)).

### Church calendar

&#x20;Lesser cycles also run in tandem with the annual ones. A weekly cycle of days prescribes a specific focus for each day in addition to others that may be observed.[^cite_note-312]

> Each day of the Weekly Cycle is dedicated to certain special memorials. Sunday is dedicated to [Christ's Resurrection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christ's_Resurrection); Monday honours the holy bodiless powers (angels, archangels, etc.); Tuesday is dedicated to the prophets and especially the greatest of the prophets, [John the Forerunner and Baptist of the Lord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_the_Baptist); Wednesday is consecrated to the Cross and recalls Judas' betrayal; Thursday honours the holy apostles and hierarchs, especially [Nicholas, Bishop of Myra in Lycia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Nicholas); Friday is also consecrated to the Cross and recalls the day of the Crucifixion; Saturday is dedicated to All Saints, especially the [Mother of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mother_of_God), and to the memory of all those who have departed this life in the hope of resurrection and eternal life.

### Church services

See also: [Canonical hours § Byzantine Rite usage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canonical_hours#Byzantine_Rite_usage)

The main service offered in the church is the [Divine Liturgy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divine_Liturgy). Most parishes offer this service on Sunday mornings and on major feast days, though it can be offered almost any day of the year.[^cite_note-313] Additional services include [Orthros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthros) and [Vespers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vespers); prayer services in the morning and evening, respectively. The celebration of the feasts are distinguished according to their various degrees of solemnity. The great feasts are celebrated with an All Night Vigil. Lesser feasts will have a vigil according to the feast's custom.[^cite_note-CS-314]

Church service books are used in divine services, like the Gospel, the Epistle, and the Psalter. These books, often called *divine service books*, were composed in accordance with the scriptures and traditions of the Church Fathers and teachers of the Orthodox Church. During fasting, in the middle of the week Holy Communion is given at all church services, without removing the characteristics of Orthodox Lent.[^cite_note-CS-314]

#### Chanting



Chanters singing on the [kliros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kliros) at the [Church of St. George](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_St._George,_Istanbul), [Patriarchate of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriarchate_of_Constantinople)

Chanting is not considered "music" by Orthodox Christians, but rather sacred melody and prayer. An [Orthodox Divine Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divine_Service_\(Eastern_Orthodoxy\)) is chanted in its entirely by the clergy, the choir, and the congregation from the start of the service to the end.[^cite_note-HD-147] Early Christian forms of chanting began with ancient Judaic traditions of chanting the Psalms. As the Church grew, many new forms of hymns began to appear.[^cite_note-315]

Liturgies contain the reading and chanting of prayers, headed by a bishop or priest.[^cite_note-CS-314] Various liturgies include different forms of chanting services (for example, the [Liturgy of St. Basil the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liturgy_of_Saint_Basil) has chanting of longer duration and the priest privately reads his prayers at the altar). For the composition of religious chant, the [Octoechos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Octoechos), an eight-[tone (mode)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mode_\(music\)) system, analogous to the [Gregorian modes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gregorian_mode) in the West, and to other ancient Christian musical systems, is used. [Byzantine music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_music) is [microtonal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Microtonal_music).

Byzantine chants are associated with the Eastern Roman Empire's period (AD 330 to 1453) and developed from Jewish and Syrian traditions in the early Christian Church. This continued to evolve until the 16th century.[^cite_note-HD-147]

Northern Slav have used simpler tonal systems evolved through the local types of [Znamenny chant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Znamenny_chant); today [Western music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_culture#Music), often with [four-part harmony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four-part_harmony), and the "tones" are simply sets of melodies. Russian liturgical chants (including those of some Ukrainian and Balkan churches) evolved from the Kievan Rus people after AD 988. Byzantine melodies adapted to the patterns of the [Old Church Slavonic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Church_Slavonic) language. In the 14th century, Russian elements began to be used in the church. By the 16th century, Russian chants had many links to the Byzantine style.[^cite_note-HD-147]

There are numerous versions and styles that are traditional and accepted and these vary a great deal between cultures.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWare1993238-316]

## Traditions

### Art and architecture

Main article: [Eastern Orthodox church architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_church_architecture)



An illustration of the traditional interior of an Orthodox church

Eastern Orthodox church architecture constitutes a distinct and recognizable family of styles within [church architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_architecture). These styles share a cluster of fundamental similarities, having been influenced by the common legacy of [Byzantine architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_architecture) from the [Eastern Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Roman_Empire). Some of these styles have become associated with the particular traditions of specific [autocephalous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autocephaly) [Eastern Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox) [patriarchates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriarchate), while others are more widely used throughout the Eastern Orthodox Church. These [architectural styles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architectural_style) have exerted substantial influence on cultures outside Eastern Orthodoxy, particularly on the architecture of [Islamic mosques](19894.md),[^cite_note-317] as well as, to some extent, on [Western churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Christianity).

### Icons and symbols

See also: [Christian symbolism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_symbolism)

#### Icons

Further information: [Icon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Icon)



*The Saviour Not Made by Hands*, an icon of the [Novgorod school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novgorod_school), c. 1100

Aspects of the [iconography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iconography) borrow from the pre-Christian [Roman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_art) and [Hellenistic art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenistic_art). [Henry Chadwick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Chadwick_\(theologian\)) wrote, "In this instinct there was a measure of truth. The representations of Christ as the Almighty Lord on his judgment throne owed something to pictures of Zeus. Portraits of the Mother of God were not wholly independent of a pagan past of venerated mother-goddesses. In the popular mind the saints had come to fill a role that had been played by heroes and deities."[^cite_note-318]

Icons can be found adorning the walls of churches and often cover the inside structure completely.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWare1993271-319] Most Eastern Orthodox homes have an area set aside for family prayer, usually an eastern facing wall, where are hung many icons. Icons have been part of Orthodox Christianity since the beginning of the church.[^cite_note-320]

#### Iconostasis

Main article: [Iconostasis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iconostasis)



Iconostasis of the [Romanian People's Salvation Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanian_People's_Salvation_Cathedral)

An *iconostasis*, also called the *templon*, is a wall of [icons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Icons) and religious paintings, separating the [nave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nave) from the [sanctuary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanctuary) in a [church](6325.md). *Iconostasis* also refers to a portable icon stand that can be placed anywhere within a church. The modern iconostasis evolved from the [Byzantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_architecture) [templon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Templon) in the 11th century. The evolution of the iconostasis probably owes a great deal to 14th-century [Hesychast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hesychasm) [mysticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mysticism) and the wood-carving genius of the [Russian Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church).[^cite_note-321]

The first ceiling-high, five-levelled Russian iconostasis was designed by [Andrey Rublyov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrey_Rublyov) in the [cathedral of the Dormition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathedral_of_the_Dormition) in [Vladimir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir,_Russia) in 1408.

#### Cross



[Greek cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Cross)



[Orthodox cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_cross)

On the Russian Orthodox cross, the small top crossbar represents the sign that [Pontius Pilate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontius_Pilate) nailed above Christ's head. It often is inscribed with an acronym, "INRI", [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_language): *Iesus Nazarenus, Rex Iudaeorum* for "[Jesus of Nazareth, King of the Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/INRI)" or "INBI", [Koine Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koine_Greek): Ἰησοῦς ὁ Ναζωραῖος ὁ βασιλεύς τῶν Ἰουδαίων for "Jesus of Nazareth, King of the Jews".[^cite_note-322]

Other crosses associated with the Eastern Orthodox Church are the more traditional single-bar crosses, budded designs, the [Greek cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_cross), the [Latin cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_cross), the [Jerusalem cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_cross) (cross pattée), [Celtic crosses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celtic_cross), and others.[^cite_note-323] A common symbolism of the slanted foot stool is the foot-rest points up, toward Heaven, on Christ's right hand-side, and downward, to Hades, on Christ's left. "Between two thieves Thy Cross did prove to be a balance of righteousness: wherefore one of them was dragged down to Hades by the weight of his blasphemy *\[the balance points downward]*, whereas the other was lightened of his transgressions unto the comprehension of theology *\[the balance points upward]*. O Christ God, glory to Thee."[^cite_note-324]

### Local customs



Shards of pottery vases on the street, after being thrown from the windows of nearby houses. A [Holy Saturday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Saturday) tradition in [Corfu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corfu).

Locality is also expressed in regional terms of churchly jurisdiction, which is often also drawn along national lines. Many Orthodox churches adopt a national title (e.g. [Albanian Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albanian_Orthodox), [Bulgarian Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgarian_Orthodox), [Antiochian Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antiochian_Orthodox), [Georgian Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_Orthodox), [Greek Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox), [Romanian Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanian_Orthodox), [Russian Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Orthodox), [Serbian Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_Orthodox), [Ukrainian Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodoxy_in_Ukraine), etc.) and this title can identify which language is used in services, which bishops preside, and which of the typica is followed by specific [congregations](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/congregation). In the Middle East, Orthodox Christians are usually referred to as *Rum* ("Roman") Orthodox, because of their historical connection with the [Eastern Roman (Byzantine) Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Roman_\(Byzantine\)_Empire).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBinns20023-325]

## Holy mysteries (sacraments)

The [Byzantine Rite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Rite) is used for the administration of the "Holy mysteries" or seven sacraments in [Eastern Orthodox Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Christianity); among these are [Holy Communion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Communion) (the most direct connection), [baptism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptism), [Chrismation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chrismation), [confession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confession_\(religion\)), [unction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unction), [matrimony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marriage_in_the_Eastern_Orthodox_Church), and [ordination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ordination), as well as [blessings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benediction), [exorcisms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exorcism_in_Christianity), and other occasions.[^cite_note-oupbr-326]

While the Catholic Church numbers seven sacraments, and many Protestant groups list two (baptism and the Eucharist) or even none, the Eastern Orthodox do not limit the number. However, for the sake of convenience, [catechisms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catechism) of the Eastern Orthodox Church will often speak of the "seven great mysteries". The term "sacrament" also properly applies to other sacred actions such as [monastic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Christian_monasticism) [tonsure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tonsure) or the blessing of [holy water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_water_in_Eastern_Christianity), and involves [fasting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_fasting#Eastern_and_Oriental_Orthodoxy), [almsgiving](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alms#In_Eastern_Christianity), or an act as simple as lighting a candle, [burning incense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_use_of_incense#Christianity), praying or asking God's blessing on food.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWare1993274–277-327]

### Baptism



An Eastern Orthodox baptism

[Baptism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptism) is the mystery which transforms the old and sinful person into a new and pure one; the old life, the sins, any mistakes made are gone and a clean slate is given. Through baptism a person is united to the [Body of Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Body_of_Christ) by becoming a member of the Eastern Orthodox Church. During the service, [water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_water_in_Eastern_Christianity) is blessed. The catechumen is fully immersed in the water three times in the name of the Trinity. This is considered to be a death of the "old man" by participation in the crucifixion and burial of Christ, and a rebirth into new life in Christ by participation in his resurrection.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWare1993277–278-328]

Properly, the mystery of baptism is administered by bishops and priests; however, [in emergencies any Eastern Orthodox Christian can baptise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emergency_baptism).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWare1993278-329]

### Chrismation

[Chrismation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chrismation) (sometimes called [confirmation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation)) is the mystery by which a baptised person is granted the gift of the [Holy Spirit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Spirit_in_Christianity) through anointing with Holy [Chrism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chrism).[^cite_note-330][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWare1993278–279-331] It is normally given immediately after baptism as part of the same service, but is also used to receive lapsed members of the Eastern Orthodox Church.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarakas198756–57-332] As baptism is a person's participation in the death and resurrection of Christ, so Chrismation is a person's participation in the coming of the Holy Spirit at [Pentecost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentecost).[^cite_note-ware1993p279-333]

A baptised and chrismated Eastern Orthodox Christian is a full member of the church and may receive the Eucharist regardless of age.[^cite_note-ware1993p279-333]

The creation of Chrism may be accomplished by any bishop at any time, but usually is done only once a year, often when a synod of bishops convenes for its annual meeting. Some autocephalous churches get their chrism from others. Anointing with it substitutes for the laying-on of hands described in the [New Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Testament), even when an instrument such as a brush is used.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarakas198757-334]

### Holy Communion (Eucharist)



Eucharistic elements prepared for the Divine Liturgy

[Holy Communion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Communion) is given only to baptised and chrismated Eastern Orthodox Christians who have prepared by fasting, prayer and confession. The priest will administer the gifts with a spoon, called a "cochlear", directly into the recipient's mouth from the chalice.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWare1993287-335] From baptism young infants and children are carried to the chalice to receive holy communion.[^cite_note-ware1993p279-333]

### Marriage

Main article: [Marriage in the Eastern Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marriage_in_the_Eastern_Orthodox_Church)



The wedding of Tsar [Nicholas II of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicholas_II_of_Russia) by [Laurits Tuxen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laurits_Tuxen)

From the Eastern Orthodox perspective, marriage is one of the holy mysteries or sacraments. As well as in many other Christian traditions, for example in Catholicism, it serves to unite a woman and a man in eternal union and love before God, with the purpose of following Christ and his Gospel and raising up a faithful, holy family through their holy union.[^cite_note-336][^cite_note-337] The church understands marriage to be the union of one man and one woman, and certain Orthodox leaders have spoken out strongly in opposition to the civil institution of [same-sex marriage](92656.md).[^cite_note-338][^cite_note-339]



Greek Orthodox wedding

Jesus said that "when they rise from the dead, they neither marry nor are given in marriage, but are like angels in heaven" (Mk 12\:25). For the Orthodox Christian this passage should not be understood to imply that Christian marriage will not remain a reality in the Kingdom, but points to the fact that relations will not be "fleshy", but "spiritual".[^cite_note-meyendorff70-340] Love between wife and husband, as an icon of relationship between Christ and church, is eternal.[^cite_note-meyendorff70-340]

The church does recognise that there are rare occasions when it is better that couples do separate, but there is no official recognition of civil divorces. For the Eastern Orthodox, to say that marriage is indissoluble means that it should not be broken, the violation of such a union, perceived as holy, being an offence resulting from either adultery or the prolonged absence of one of the partners. Thus, permitting remarriage is an act of compassion of the church towards sinful man.[^cite_note-341]

### Holy orders



Eastern Orthodox [subdeacon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subdeacon) being ordained to the [diaconate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diaconate). The [bishop](4092.md) has placed his [omophorion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omophorion) and right hand on the head of the candidate and is reading the *Prayer of [Cheirotonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_laying_on_of_hands)*.

Widowed priests and [deacons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deacon) may not remarry and it is common for such members of the clergy to retire to a monastery (see [clerical celibacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clerical_celibacy)). This is also true of widowed wives of clergy, who do not remarry and become nuns when their children are grown. Only men are allowed to receive [holy orders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_orders), although [deaconesses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deaconess) had both liturgical and pastoral functions within the church.[^cite_note-Karras-342] Women had been ordained as deacons or deaconesses in the [Byzantine Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodoxy) through the 9th century CE after which the practice fell into disuse.[^cite_note-343][^cite_note-344]

In 2004, the Holy Synod of the [Church of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_Greece) voted to permit the consecration of women as 'deaconesses', without using the word 'ordination,' but only within monastic communities.[^cite_note-345] In 2016, the Holy Synod of the [Greek Orthodox Patriarchate of Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Patriarchate_of_Alexandria) voted to reinstate the [female diaconate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deaconess); in the following year, it ordained six sub-deaconesses in the [Democratic Republic of Congo](76762.md). In 2024 the Patriarchate ordained its first female deacon, [Angelic Molen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angelic_Molen), in Zimbabwe, making her the first female deacon in the modern history of the Eastern Orthodox Church.[^cite_note-346][^cite_note-347][^cite_note-348] This move was met with criticism from other autocephalous Orthodox church leaders and clergy, including the [Antiochian Orthodox Christian Archdiocese of North America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antiochian_Orthodox_Christian_Archdiocese_of_North_America)'s [Metropolitan Saba Esber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saba_Esber) and Archpriest John Whiteford of the [Russian Orthodox Church Outside of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church_Outside_of_Russia). Critics argued that the decision was made without sufficient pan-Orthodox consultation and raised concerns about its theological and ecclesiological implications. Whiteford also criticized some supporters of the restoration of the female diaconate for what he described as having broader ideological aims that went beyond the historical role of deaconesses in the Eastern Orthodox Church.[^cite_note-349][^cite_note-350]

## Interfaith relations



The consecration of [Reginald Heber Weller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reginald_Heber_Weller) as an Anglican bishop at the [Cathedral of St. Paul the Apostle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Paul's_Cathedral,_Fond_du_Lac) in the [Episcopal Diocese of Fond du Lac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Episcopal_Diocese_of_Fond_du_Lac), with Anthony Kozlowski of the [Polish National Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_National_Catholic_Church) and [Tikhon, then Bishop of the Aleutians and Alaska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriarch_Tikhon_of_Moscow) (along with his chaplains [John Kochurov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Kochurov) and [Sebastian Dabovich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sebastian_Dabovich)) of the [Russian Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church) present



[Pope Francis](1687680.md) and [Ecumenical Patriarch Bartholomew I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecumenical_Patriarch_Bartholomew_I) in the [Church of the Holy Sepulchre](7810.md), Jerusalem, 2014

### Relations with other Christians

In 1920, the Ecumenical Patriarchate of Constantinople, published an encyclical "addressed 'To all the Churches of Christ, wherever they may be', urging closer co-operation among separated Christians, and suggesting a 'League of Churches', parallel to the newly founded [League of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/League_of_Nations)".[^cite_note-Ware1993-351] This gesture was instrumental in the foundation of the [World Council of Churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Council_of_Churches) (WCC);[^cite_note-Fuchs2008-352] as such, almost all Eastern Orthodox churches are members of the WCC and "Orthodox ecclesiastics and theologians serve on its committees".[^cite_note-Benz2008-353] In 1922, the Ecumenical Patriarchate declared that the [Anglican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglican_Communion), [Armenian Apostolic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_Apostolic_Church), [Old Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Catholic_Church), and [Roman Catholic](606848.md) churches possessed "the same validity" of Holy Orders and apostolic succession.[^cite_note-354][^cite_note-355] Following the Ecumenical Patriarchate were the autocephalous churches of Alexandria, Jerusalem, Romania, and Cyprus as well as the autonomous church of Sinai.[^cite_note-356][^cite_note-357] However, in 1948, the Russian Orthodox Church declined to recognize the validity of Anglican Orders.[^cite_note-358] [Kallistos Ware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kallistos_Ware), a British metropolitan bishop of the Orthodox Church, has stated that [ecumenism](146656.md) "is important for Orthodoxy: it has helped to force the various Orthodox churches out of their comparative isolation, making them meet one another and enter into a living contact with non-Orthodox Christians."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWare1993322-359]

[Hilarion Alfeyev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hilarion_Alfeyev), then the Metropolitan of Volokolamsk and head of external relations for the Moscow Patriarchate of the Russian Orthodox Church, stated that Orthodox and [Evangelical Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelical_Protestant) Christians share the same positions on "such issues as [abortion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pro-life), the [family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family_values#Conservative_definitions), and [marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_views_on_marriage)" and desire "vigorous grassroots engagement" between the two [Christian communions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communion_\(Christian\)) on such issues.[^cite_note-Communions-360]

In that regard, the differences between the Catholic and Eastern Orthodox communions have not been improved in any relevant way. Dogmatic and [liturgical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liturgical) polarities have been significant, even and especially in recent times. A pertinent point of contention between the monarchically papal, administratively centralised Catholic Church and the decentralised confederation of Orthodox churches is the theological significance of the [Virgin Mary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virgin_Mary).[^cite_note-361] During his visit to [Georgia](48768.md) in October 2016, [Pope Francis](1687680.md) was snubbed by most Orthodox Christians as he led mass before a practically empty [Mikheil Meskhi Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikheil_Meskhi_Stadium) in [Tbilisi](42298.md).[^cite_note-362]

The Oriental Orthodox Churches are not in [communion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communion_\(Christian\)) with the Eastern Orthodox Church, despite their similar names. Slow dialogue towards restoring communion between the two churches began in the mid-20th century,[^cite_note-363] and, notably, in the 19th century, when the Greek Patriarch in Egypt had to absent himself from the country for a long period of time; he left his church under the guidance of the Coptic [Pope Cyril IV of Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Cyril_IV_of_Alexandria).[^cite_note-364]

In 2019, the Primate of the OCU [Metropolitan of Kyiv and All Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_of_Kyiv_and_all_Ukraine) [Epiphanius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epiphanius_of_Kyiv) stated that "theoretically" the [Orthodox Church of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Church_of_Ukraine) and the [Ukrainian Greek Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_Greek_Catholic_Church) could in the future unite into a united church around the Kyiv throne.[^cite_note-365] In 2019, the Primate of the UGCC, [Major Archbishop of Kyiv-Galicia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lists_of_leaders_of_the_Ukrainian_Greek_Catholic_Church) [Sviatoslav](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sviatoslav_Shevchuk), stated that every effort should be made to restore the original unity of the Kyivan Church in its Orthodox and Catholic branches, saying that the restoration of Eucharistic communion between [Rome](606848.md) and [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecumenical_Patriarchate_of_Constantinople) is not a utopia.[^cite_note-366]

Notwithstanding certain overtures by both Catholic and Eastern Orthodox leaders, the majority of Orthodox Christians, as well as Catholics, are not in favour of communion between their churches, with only a median of 35 per cent and 38 per cent, respectively, claiming support.[^cite_note-ReferenceA-144]

### Relations with Islam



The [Constantinople Massacre of April 1821](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople_Massacre_of_1821): a religious persecution of the Greek population of [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople) under the Ottomans. Patriarch [Gregory V of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gregory_V_of_Constantinople) was executed.

In 2007, Metropolitan [Alfeyev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hilarion_Alfeyev) expressed the possibility of peaceful coexistence between Islam and [Christianity](5211.md) in Russia, as the two religions have never had religious wars in Russia.[^cite_note-367]

## Constituencies

See also: [List of the current autocephalous Eastern Orthodox churches and their dependencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church_organization)

The various [autocephalous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autocephalous) and autonomous [synods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synods) of the Eastern Orthodox Church are distinct in terms of administration and local culture, but for the most part exist in [full communion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Full_communion) with one another. However, there have been varying instances in the history of the Eastern Orthodox Church where communion has been broken between member churches for short periods, particularly over autocephaly issues or disagreements over [ecumenism](146656.md) with other Christian denominations.[^cite_note-368][^cite_note-369][^cite_note-370][^cite_note-371] In addition, some schismatic churches not in any communion exist, with all three groups identifying as Eastern Orthodox.



The [Pan-Orthodox Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Orthodox_Council), Kolymvari, Crete, Greece, June 2016

Another group of non-mainstream Eastern Orthodox Christians are referred [True Orthodoxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/True_Orthodoxy) or [Old Calendarists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Calendarists); they are those who, without authority from their parent churches, have continued to use the old [Julian calendar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julian_calendar), and split from their parent church.[^cite_note-\:13-372]

The [Russian Orthodox Church Outside Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church_Outside_Russia) (ROCOR) has [united in 2007 with the Moscow Patriarchate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Act_of_Canonical_Communion_with_the_Moscow_Patriarchate); these two churches had separated from each other in the 1920s due to the subjection of the latter to the hostile [Soviet regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union).[^cite_note-373]

Another group, called the [Old Believers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Believers), [separated in 1666](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raskol) from the official Russian Orthodox Church as a protest against church rite reforms introduced by [Patriarch Nikon of Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriarch_Nikon_of_Moscow).[^cite_note-374]

### Main communion



[Cathedral of Evangelismos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathedral_of_Evangelismos), [Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandria)

The Eastern Orthodox Church is a [communion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Full_communion) of 15 [autocephalous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autocephalous)—that is, administratively completely independent—regional churches,[^cite_note-375] plus the [Orthodox Church in America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Church_in_America) and two [Ukrainian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Church_of_Ukraine) [Orthodox Churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_Orthodox_Church_\(Moscow_Patriarchate\)). The Orthodox Church in America is recognised as autocephalous only by the Russian, Bulgarian, Georgian, Polish and Czech-Slovak churches. In December 2018, representatives of two unrecognised Ukrainian Orthodox churches, along with two [metropolitans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_bishop) of the recognised, but self-declared autocephalous [Ukrainian Orthodox Church of the Moscow Patriarchate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_Orthodox_Church_of_the_Moscow_Patriarchate), [proclaimed the formation of the unified Orthodox Church of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unification_council_of_the_Eastern_Orthodox_churches_of_Ukraine). On 5 January 2019, the Orthodox Church of Ukraine received its *[tomos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tomos_\(Eastern_Orthodox_Church\))* of autocephaly (decree which defines the conditions of a church's independence) from the Ecumenical Patriarchate and thus received a place in the [diptych](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diptych#Ecclesiastical).



[Patriarchate of Peć](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriarchate_of_Pe%C4%87_\(monastery\)) in [Kosovo](17391.md), the seat of the [Serbian Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_Orthodox_Church) from the 14th century, when its status was upgraded into a patriarchate

Each church has defined geographical boundaries of its jurisdiction and is ruled by its council of bishops or synod presided by a senior bishop–its [primate](24672.md) (or first hierarch). The primate may carry the honorary title of patriarch, metropolitan (in the Slavic tradition) or archbishop (in the Greek tradition).

Each regional church consists of constituent [eparchies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eparchies) (or dioceses) ruled by a bishop. Some churches have given an eparchy or group of eparchies varying degrees of [autonomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomy_\(Eastern_Orthodoxy\)) (self-government). Such autonomous churches maintain varying levels of dependence on their [mother church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mother_church), usually defined in a [*tomos*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tomos_\(Eastern_Orthodox_Church\)) or other document of autonomy.

Below is a list of the 15 autocephalous Orthodox churches forming the main body of Orthodox Christianity, all of which are titled equal to each other, but the Ecumenical Patriarchate is titled the *first among equals*. Based on the definitions, the list is in the order of precedence and alphabetical order where necessary, with some of their constituent autonomous churches and exarchates listed as well. The liturgical title of the primate is in italics.

- [Ecumenical Patriarchate of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecumenical_Patriarchate_of_Constantinople) (*Archbishop of Constantinople-New Rome and First Among Equals Patriarch*)&#x20;

  - Autonomous [Orthodox Church of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Church_of_Finland) (*Archbishop of Helsinki and All Finland*, formerly *Archbishop of Karelia and All Finland*)

  - Self-governing Orthodox [Church of Crete](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_Crete) (*Archbishop of Crete*)

  - Self-governing [monastic community of Mount Athos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monastic_community_of_Mount_Athos)

  - Self-governing [Orthodox Church of Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Church_of_Korea) (*Metropolitan of Seoul and All Korea*)

  - [Exarchate of Lithuania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exarchate_of_Lithuania)

- [Greek Orthodox Patriarchate of Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Patriarchate_of_Alexandria) (*the Pope and Patriarch of the Great City of [Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandria), [Libya](17633.md), [Pentapolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentapolis_\(North_Africa\)), [Ethiopia](187749.md), all the land of [Egypt](8087628.md), and all Africa*)

- [Greek Orthodox Patriarchate of Antioch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Patriarchate_of_Antioch) (*Patriarch of Antioch and all the East*)&#x20;

  - Self-governing [Archdiocese of North America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antiochian_Orthodox_Christian_Archdiocese_of_North_America) (Metropolitan of North America and Archbishop of New York)

- [Greek Orthodox Patriarchate of Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Patriarchate_of_Jerusalem) (*Patriarch of the Holy City of Jerusalem and all Holy Land, Syria, Arabia, beyond the Jordan River, Cana of Galilee, and Sacred Zion*)&#x20;

  - Autonomous [Church of Mount Sinai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_Sinai) (*Archbishop of Choreb, Sinai, and Raitha*)

- [Russian Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church) (*Patriarch of Moscow and all Russia*)&#x20;

  - Autonomous [Orthodox Church in Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Church_in_Japan) (*Archbishop of Tokyo and Metropolitan of All Japan*)

  - [Exarchate of Belarus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belarusian_Orthodox_Church) (*Metropolitan of Minsk and Slutsk, Patriarchal Exarch of All Belarus*)

  - Self-governing [Russian Orthodox Church Outside Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church_Outside_Russia) (*Metropolitan of Eastern America and New York, First Hierarch of the Russian church abroad*)

- [Serbian Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_Orthodox_Church) (*Archbishop of Peć, Metropolitan of Belgrade and Karlovci, and Serbian Patriarch*)

- [Bulgarian Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgarian_Orthodox_Church) (*Metropolitan of Sofia and Patriarch of All Bulgaria*)

- [Romanian Orthodox Church](384458.md) (*Archbishop of Bucharest, Metropolitan of Muntenia and Dobrudja, Locum Tenens of the Throne of Caesarea of Cappadocia, and Patriarch of Romania*)&#x20;

  - Autonomous [Romanian Orthodox Metropolis of the Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanian_Orthodox_Metropolis_of_the_Americas) (*Romanian Orthodox Archbishop of the United States of America and Romanian Orthodox Metropolitan of the Americas*)

- [Georgian Orthodox Church](433340.md) (*Catholicos-Patriarch of All Georgia, the Archbishop of Mtskheta-Tbilisi and Metropolitan bishop of Abkhazia and Pitsunda*)

- [Church of Cyprus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_Cyprus) (*Archbishop of New Justiniana and all Cyprus*)

- [Church of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_Greece) (*Archbishop of Athens and all Greece*)

- [Albanian Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albanian_Orthodox_Church) (*Archbishop of Tirana, Durres and all Albania*)

- [Polish Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_Orthodox_Church) (*Metropolitan of Warsaw and all Poland* or *Archbishop of Warsaw and Metropolitan of All Poland)[^cite_note-377]*

- [Orthodox Church of the Czech Lands and Slovakia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Church_of_the_Czech_Lands_and_Slovakia) (*Archbishop of Prague, the Metropolitan of Czech lands and Slovakia or the Archbishop of Presov, the Metropolitan of Czech lands and Slovakia*)

- [Macedonian Orthodox Church – Ohrid Archbishopric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macedonian_Orthodox_Church_%E2%80%93_Ohrid_Archbishopric) (*Metropolitan of Skopje and Archbishop of Ohrid and Macedonia and of Justiniana Prima*)

Within the main body of Eastern Orthodoxy there are unresolved internal issues as to the autonomous or autocephalous status or legitimacy of the following Orthodox churches, particularly between those stemming from the Russian Orthodox or Constantinopolitan churches:

- [Orthodox Church in America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Church_in_America) (*Archbishop of Washington, Metropolitan of All America and Canada*) – Autocephaly not recognised by the Ecumenical Patriarchate.

- Self-governing [Estonian Apostolic Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Apostolic_Orthodox_Church) (*Metropolitan of Tallinn and all Estonia*) – Recognised only by the Ecumenical Patriarchate, opposed only by the Russian Orthodox Church.

- Self-governing [Estonian Orthodox Church of the Moscow Patriarchate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Orthodox_Church_of_the_Moscow_Patriarchate) (*Metropolitan of Tallinn and all Estonia*) – Declared itself autocephalous in 2024; not recognised by the Ecumenical Patriarchate.

- Autonomous [Bessarabian Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bessarabian_Orthodox_Church) in Moldova (*Archbishop of Chișinău, Metropolitan of Bessarabia and Exarch of the Territories*) of the Romanian Orthodox Church – Territory claimed by the Russian Orthodox Church.

- Autonomous [Moldovan Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moldovan_Orthodox_Church) (*Metropolitan of Chișinău and all Moldova*) of the Russian Orthodox Church – Jurisdiction disputed by the Romanian Orthodox Church.

- [Orthodox Church of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Church_of_Ukraine) (*Metropolitan of Kyiv and All Ukraine*) – Recognised by the Ecumenical Patriarchate, Church of Greece, Church of Cyprus, and Patriarchate of Alexandria

  [^cite_note-378]

  [^cite_note-379]

  [^cite_note-380]

  [^cite_note-381]

  [^cite_note-382]

  [^cite_note-383]

  &#x20;as of October 2020, opposed by the Russian, Antiochian, Czech and Slovak, Serbian and Polish Orthodox Churches, and the Orthodox Church in America.

  [^cite_note-384]

  [^cite_note-385]

  [^cite_note-386]

- [Ukrainian Orthodox Church (Moscow Patriarchate)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_Orthodox_Church_\(Moscow_Patriarchate\)), self-governing by declaration which later was approved and recognised by the Georgian Orthodox Church

  [^cite_note-387]

  &#x20;– jurisdiction disputed with the [Orthodox Church of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Church_of_Ukraine), which nearly all Churches continued to recognise as part of ROC.

- [Latvian Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latvian_Orthodox_Church) (*Metropolitan of Riga and all Latvia*) – holding autocephaly prior to 1941, forcibly integrated in 1941 as a result of the [Soviet occupation and annexation of the Baltic states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_occupation_of_the_Baltic_states_\(1940\)) to become a self-governing part of the [Russian Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church), with the Ecumenical Patriarchate accepting this situation in 1978; in 2022, the Latvian Parliament (the [Saeima](499765.md)) declared the restoration of autocephaly of the LOC from the ROC, due to security reasons.

### Traditionalist groups

#### True Orthodox

Main article: [Eastern Orthodox Church organization § True Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church_organization#True_Orthodox)

[True Orthodoxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/True_Orthodoxy) has been separated from the mainstream communion over issues of ecumenism and calendar reform since the 1920s.[^cite_note-politika2-388] The movement rejects the Ecumenical Patriarchate of Constantinople, the Moscow Patriarchate, and all churches which are in communion with them, accusing them of heresy and placing themselves under bishops who do the same thing. They adhere to the use of the [Julian calendar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julian_calendar), claiming that the [calendar reform in the 1920s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revised_Julian_calendar) is in contradiction with the ecumenical councils. There is no official communion of True Orthodox; and they often are local groups and are limited to a specific bishop or locality.

#### Old calendarists

Main article: [Old Calendarists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Calendarists)

[Old Calendarists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Calendarists) are traditionalist groups of [Eastern Orthodox Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Christians) that separated from mainstream Eastern Orthodox churches because [some of the latter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Calendarists) [adopted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Constantinople_\(1923\)) the [revised Julian calendar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revised_Julian_calendar) while Old Calendarists remained committed to the [Julian calendar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julian_calendar).*[^cite_note-\:0-389][^cite_note-\:13-372]* Old Calendarists are not in [communion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Full_communion) with any mainstream Eastern Orthodox churches.[^cite_note-390] "Old Calendarists" is another name for the [True Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/True_Orthodox) movement in [Romania](25445.md), [Bulgaria](3415.md), [Greece](12108.md) and [Cyprus](5593.md).[^cite_note-\:12-391] In 1999, it was estimated that "\[t]here are probably over one million Old Calendarists in Romania, somewhat fewer in Greece, and considerably fewer in Bulgaria, Cyprus, and the Eastern Orthodox [diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diaspora)."[^cite_note-\:2-392]

#### Old Believers

Main article: [Old Believers § Old Believer churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Believers#Old_Believer_churches)



Traditional [Paschal](9325.md) procession by [Russian Orthodox Old-Rite Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Old-Rite_Church)

[Old Believers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Believers) are groups which do not accept the liturgical reforms which were carried out within the Russian Orthodox Church by [Patriarch Nikon of Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriarch_Nikon_of_Moscow) in the 17th century. Although all of the groups of Old Believers emerged as a result of opposition to the Nikonian reforms, they do not constitute a single monolithic body. Despite their emphasis on invariable adherence to the pre-Nikonian traditions, the Old Believers feature a great diversity of groups which profess different interpretations of church tradition and they are often not in communion with each other (some groups even practise re-baptism before admitting a member of another group into their midst).

### Churches not in communion with other churches

Main article: [Organization of the Eastern Orthodox Church § Churches that are not recognised despite wanting to](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organization_of_the_Eastern_Orthodox_Church#Churches_that_are_not_recognised_despite_wanting_to)

Churches existing with irregular or unresolved canonical status are entities that have carried out [episcopal consecrations outside of the norms of canon law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Episcopi_vagantes), or whose bishops have been excommunicated by one of the mainstream autocephalous churches. These include nationalist and other schismatic bodies such as the [Abkhazian Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abkhazian_Orthodox_Church) and [American Orthodox Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Orthodox_Catholic_Church).

[^cite_note-148]: &#x20;[Rus'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rus'_\(region\)) is a region inhabited by [East Slavs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Slavs) who were once ruled by princes from the [Rurik dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rurik_dynasty). This term refers to the [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages), in contrast to the more recent (15th century) term "Russia". See also: [Names of Rus', Russia and Ruthenia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Names_of_Rus',_Russia_and_Ruthenia).

[^cite_note-213]: &#x20;According to Roman Lunkin in an interview about the 2012 survey published by Среда (*_Sreda_*), about 40% of the [Russian Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Federation) population is Orthodox. However, only 5% belong to a parish or regularly attend Divine Liturgy. Lunkin said that this was long known by experts but a myth persists that 80–90% of the population is Orthodox.<sup>[\[209\]](#cite_note-211)</sup> According to *The World Factbook* 2006 estimate, 15–20% are practising Russian Orthodox but there is a large populations of non-practising believers.<sup>[\[210\]](#cite_note-CIAWeb-212)</sup>

[^cite_note-216]: &#x20;Data are estimated, there are no census figures available, Greece is said to be 98% Orthodox by CIA, but additional studies found only 60–80% believe in God, if true, then no more than 80% may be Orthodox.

[^cite_note-217]: &#x20;With an absolute majority in the subnational entity of [Republika Srpska](347422.md).

[^cite_note-231]: &#x20;According to Alexei Krindatch, "the total number of Orthodox parishes" increased by 16% from 2000 to 2010 in the United States, from this, he wrote that Orthodox Churches are growing.<sup>[\[225\]](#cite_note-Krindatch2010-230)</sup><sup>: 2 </sup> Krindatch did not provide figures about any change in the membership over that same period in his 2010 highlight.

[^cite_note-234]: &#x20;According to Oliver Herbel, in *Turning to Tradition*, the 2008 US Religious Landscape Survey "suggests that if there is growth, it is statistically insignificant."<sup>[\[226\]](#cite_note-Herbel2014-232)</sup><sup>: 9 </sup> The 2014 US Religious Landscape Survey also shows, within the survey's ±9.2% margin of sampling error corresponding to the sample size of the *Orthodox Christian* category being 186 people, a statistically insignificant decline within the category "Orthodox Christians" as the percentage of population from 2007 to 2014.<sup>[\[227\]](#cite_note-PewRLS2015-233)</sup><sup>: 4, 21, 36, 93 </sup> But only 53% of people who were Orthodox Christian as children still self identify as Orthodox Christian in 2014.<sup>[\[227\]](#cite_note-PewRLS2015-233)</sup><sup>: 39 </sup> The *Orthodox Christian* category "is most heavily made up of immigrants and the children of immigrants."<sup>[\[227\]](#cite_note-PewRLS2015-233)</sup><sup>: 53 </sup>

[^cite_note-134]: &#x20;["History"](https://www.uocofusa.org/history). *Ukrainian Orthodox Church of the USA*. [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20250307181610/https://www.uocofusa.org/history) from the original on 2025-03-07. Retrieved 2025-03-07. In 1928 a second group of Ukrainian Orthodox faithful in the USA initiated a movement toward Orthodoxy. Because of the questions surrounding the status of Archbishop John, the group hesitated in affiliating itself with his already established jurisdiction even though it was thriving. The first Sobor of this group met in Allentown, PA in the spring of 1929 and established itself as the Ukrainian Orthodox Church in America. Rev. Dr. Joseph Zuk was elected as administrator to organize the diocese and at its second Sobor of 1931 in New York City he was elected as its first Bishop. Two hierarchs of the Syrian Orthodox Church in the USA consecrated Bishop Zuk in 1932 at St. Volodymyr Cathedral, on 14th Street in New York City. Unfortunately, Bishop Joseph lived less than two years following his consecration.

[^cite_note-146]: &#x20;["Statement of the Holy Synod of the Russian Orthodox Church 8 November 2000: Russian Orthodox Church"](https://web.archive.org/web/20180612143211/https://mospat.ru/archive/en/2000/11/se011081/). *Department for External Church Relations of the Russian Orthodox Church*. 12 November 2000. Archived from [the original](https://mospat.ru/archive/en/2000/11/se011081/) on 12 June 2018. Retrieved 2018-11-01. Patriarch Bartholomew issued an 'Act' on 20 February 1996 on the renewal of the 1923 Tomos of Patriarch Meletius IV and on the establishment of the 'Autonomous Orthodox Estonian Metropolia' on the territory of Estonia. Temporal administration was entrusted to Archbishop John of Karelia and All Finland. A schismatic group headed by the suspended clergymen was accepted into canonical communion. Thus the schism in Estonia became a reality.\
    \
    On 23 February 1996, in response to the one-sided and illegal actions of Patriarch Bartholomew the Holy Synod of the Russian Orthodox Church resolved to recognise them 'as schismatic and compelling our Church to suspend canonical and Eucharistic communion with the Patriarchate of Constantinople... and to omit the name of the Patriarch of Constantinople in the diptych of the Primates of the Local Orthodox Churches.'

[^cite_note-160]: &#x20;["Statement by the Holy Synod of the Russian Orthodox Church concerning the encroachment of the Patriarchate of Constantinople on the canonical territory of the Russian Church"](https://mospat.ru/en/2018/10/15/news165263/). Russian Orthodox Church. 15 October 2018. [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20190502133723/https://mospat.ru/en/2018/10/15/news165263/) from the original on 2 May 2019. Retrieved 2019-07-16. To admit into communion schismatics and a person anathematized in other Local Church with all the 'bishops' and 'clergy' consecrated by him, the encroachment on somebody else's canonical regions, the attempt to abandon its own historical decisions and commitments – all this leads the Patriarchate of Constantinople beyond the canonical space and, to our great grief, makes it impossible for us to continue the Eucharistic community with its hierarch, clergy and laity. From now on until the Patriarchate of Constantinople's rejection of its anti-canonical decisions, it is impossible for all the clergy of the Russian Orthodox Church to concelebrate with the clergy of the Church of Constantinople and for the laity to participate in sacraments administered in its churches.

[^cite_note-196]: &#x20;"Gsell, Most Rev. Francis Xavier (27 Oct. 1872–12 July 1960), RC Bishop of Darwin, 1938–49; Titular Bishop of Paris; assistant at the Pontifical throne, Rome, 1951", *Who Was Who*, Oxford University Press, 1 December 2007, [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.1093/ww/9780199540884.013.u238027](https://doi.org/10.1093%2Fww%2F9780199540884.013.u238027) "Everywhere following the decrees of the Holy Fathers, and aware of the recently recognized Canon of the one hundred and fifty most God-beloved Bishops who convened during the reign of Theodosius the Great of pious memory, who became emperor in the imperial city of Constantinople otherwise known as New Rome; we too decree and vote the same things in regard to the privileges and priorities of the most holy Church of that same Constantinople and New Rome. And this is in keeping with the fact that the Fathers naturally enough granted the priorities to the throne of Old Rome on account of her being the imperial capital. And motivated by the same object and aim the one hundred and fifty most God-beloved Bishops have accorded the like priorities to the most holy throne of New Rome, with good reason deeming that the city which is the seat of an empire, and of a senate, and is equal to old imperial Rome in respect of other privileges and priorities, should be magnified also as she is in respect of ecclesiastical affairs, as coming next after her, or as being second to her."

[^cite_note-Ware1993-351]: &#x20;[Ware 1993](#CITEREFWare1993), p. 322 "From the beginning of the twentieth century the Ecumenical Patriarchate has shown a special concern for Christian reconciliation. At his accession in 1902, Patriarch Joachim III sent an encyclical letter to all the autocephalous Orthodox Churches, asking in particular for their opinion on relations with other Christian bodies. In January 1920 the Ecumenical Patriarchate followed this up with a bold and prophetic letter addressed 'To all the Churches of Christ, wherever they may be', urging closer co-operation among separated Christians, and suggesting a 'League of Churches', parallel to the newly founded League of Nations. Many of the ideas in this letter anticipate subsequent developments in the WCC. Constantinople, along with several of the other Orthodox Churches, was represented at the Faith and Order Conferences at Lausanne in 1927 and at Edinburgh in 1937. The Ecumenical Patriarchate also participated in the first Assembly of the WCC at Amsterdam in 1948, and has been a consistent supporter of the work of the WCC ever since."

[^cite_note-Fuchs2008-352]: &#x20;Fuchs, Lorelei F. (2008). *Koinonia and the Quest for an Ecumenical Ecclesiology*. Wm. B. Eerdmans Publishing. p. 162. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-0-8028-4023-3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-0-8028-4023-3). Addressed "to all the Churches of Christ, wheresoever they be", the letter of the Ecumenical Patriarchate opens the words anticipating the spirit of the ecclesial bodies which would later form the World Council of Churches.

[^cite_note-Benz2008-353]: &#x20;Benz, Ernst (31 July 2008). *The Eastern Orthodox Church*. Transaction Publishers. p. 197. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-0-202-36575-6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-0-202-36575-6). A large number of Orthodox Churches are members of the World Council of Churches; Orthodox ecclesiastics and theologians serve on its committees and attend its conferences.
